- **Flagged, not acted on:** Partner arguably has the same low-churn profile as Goal (you don't usually switch who you're talking to mid-conversation), so the same Start-time-plus-header-chip placement could eventually apply to it too — left on the Express Panel for now since it is shipped and working; revisit both together later if desired.

---

## Recording indicator / partner awareness — near-term scope + deferred camera-LED (Ken, July 18 2026)

Full write-up lives in the **Security Remediation Backlog → SEC-7** entry (the geometry reality, the honest scope of capture, the agreed near-term build scope, and the deferred rear-camera-LED to-do). Summary: the tablet screen faces the *user*, not the partner, so on-screen indicators reach the partner only on a glance; the reliable partner-reachable signal is **audible**. **Agreed near-term build (not yet built):** (1) a prominent on-screen live-mic state (upgrade from the subtle red Listen button); (2) a **start-of-listening chime** (short synthesized Web Audio tone on the transition into capture — the single choke point `ui.setListenButtonState(true)` — Settings toggle, default on, NOT continuous); (3) a printed "This device listens and speaks for me" device card (beta-kit / manual deliverable, not code). **Deferred future to-do:** slow-flashing the rear-facing camera LED / torch toward the partner (capability-gated progressive enhancement — torch control is essentially Android-Chrome-only and unlikely on Surface; adds a camera-permission prompt + battery/thermal cost — build only after on-device testing, opt-in, default off). SEC-7 is **beta-gated** (load-bearing once a real, unaware partner is transcribed through a cloud STT service).

---

## Practice Mode — full plan (Ken, July 18 2026; DECIDED, NOT built)

The "Practice Communication Partner" from Architecture Overview §8. The AI plays the partner: it authors the partner's side of a scenario (spoken via TTS, shown in the transcript), and the user selects responses exactly as in a real conversation. The Strategic Assessment (§4) flags a **minimal** Practice Mode as the highest-value unbuilt feature for the alpha→beta stage — it lets testers learn the loop without spending a real partner's patience, exercises the system in acoustically clean conditions (separating UI/loop bugs from acoustic bugs), and de-risks the first real conversation (the moment most likely to decide whether a tester continues).

**Why it's a modest build — it reuses the entire Phase-1 pipeline; only the mic is replaced by the AI.** Verified against the code (July 18 2026): `handleSilencePeriod(text)` in `app.js` is **pure text processing — it never touches the mic** (sets `currentPartnerText`, mirrors the raw line to the transcript via `logPartnerInterim`, `engine.partnerSpeaking(text)`, `placeholders.arm()`, `generateOptions(text)`). So AI-authored partner text can flow through the exact same path a heard utterance takes, and the user's response cards come out identically. Three small integration points:

1. **Author the partner line + feed it in.** New `llm.generatePartnerUtterance(scenario, history, context)` produces the partner's next line from the scenario persona + conversation so far. The app speaks it (TTS, distinct voice — see below), displays it as a partner turn, then hands the text to the normal generation path (reuse `handleSilencePeriod`'s body, or call an equivalent that skips the STT-specific bits). **Cost: two LLM calls per turn** — one to author the partner line, one to classify it + generate the user's options (the second is the normal `generateResponses` path). Accepted: practice is deliberate and user-funded; reusing the pipeline verbatim is worth the extra call. (A future optimization could have the partner-authoring call also emit the classification to save the second call, but that diverges from "reuse the pipeline" — not for the first build.)
2. **Fork the post-selection step.** `handleResponseSelected` currently ends by resuming the mic (`startFreshListening` / `resumeOrIdle` / `offerClosings`). In practice mode it instead cues the next partner line — but *through the same listen-gate* (see the Start-Listening design below), so it's `resumeOrIdle`-shaped, not a parallel path.
3. **Bypass STT.** No mic in practice mode → **no echo problem** (the app speaks the partner line into a room where nothing is listening). This is precisely why practice mode is acoustically clean and separates loop/UI problems from acoustic ones.

Everything else — placeholders (rehearsed as-is), the palette, Reframe, "In my own words," the transcript, End conversation — is untouched.

**"Start Listening" IS the partner cue — reinforcing the real listening discipline (Ken, July 18 2026; the key design decision).** Rather than auto-playing the partner on entry, Practice Mode maps the **Start Listening** button (same button, same `mic` icon, same red/selected "capturing" state) to *"cue the AI partner to speak."* It does **not** activate the mic — under the hood it calls the practice-partner advance instead of `stt.startListening()` — but it rehearses the exact motor step new users most need to internalize (*"when it's the partner's turn, I press this"*). Crucially, it honors the **same gating logic as the real app** (`manualListenArmed` + `loadAutoRelisten`, v0.2.32), so practice trains the actual discipline, not a practice-only shortcut:
- **Entry waits** — picking a scenario does not auto-speak the partner. The *first* action is the user pressing Start Listening, which arms the session (`manualListenArmed = true`, exactly as real) → the AI partner speaks its opening line → options appear.
- **Between turns it mirrors the user's own auto-resume setting** — after the user responds, the same `resumeOrIdle` gate runs: auto-resume on → the next partner line fires automatically (rehearsing hands-free flow); off → the button goes idle and the user must press Start Listening again to get the next partner line. The user practices *their own configured* rhythm and learns what auto-resume does.
- **Same visual** — pressing shows the capture (red/selected) state while the partner "speaks," held until the user responds; only the underlying action differs.
- Applies regardless of who opens: if the scenario `opensWith: 'user'`, the user opens via **Start conversation** (existing opener flow) and the AI reacts; the partner's *subsequent* turns are still cued by Start Listening. So practice always waits for Start Listening to produce a partner line — simple and consistent.
- **Chime:** the start-of-listening chime (recording-indicator work above) fires on this same transition. In practice there's no partner to alert, but let it play — rehearsing the full sensory experience ("press it, it beeps, the partner talks") is part of the point; trivially suppressible later if it grates.

**Decisions (Ken, July 18 2026, via AskUserQuestion):**
- **Scope of first build: small bundled library + picker.** ~5 scenarios (one per §8 category — Social / Practical / Professional / Medical / Personal) with a scenario-picker modal. The picker is cheap; scenario variety is the value. Custom-scenario editing deferred (a later increment; when built, mirror the other user-owned sets — bundled defaults + data-folder-portable editor with the v0.2.25 "file in folder wins" + seeded-watermark reconciliation).
- **Distinct partner voice.** Extend `tts.speak(text, opts)` with an optional voice override + a "partner voice" Settings value, so the user can tell partner-vs-self apart aurally. Modest change — `getVoices()` infrastructure already exists; today `speak(text)` uses the single selected voice.
- **Save practice conversations, flagged as practice.** Stamp the conversation as `Practice: <scenario>` (via the `partnerStamp()` seam) so it's reviewable but distinguishable from real conversations, and supports future relive-and-critique on practice runs.
- **Launcher on the pre-start screen: Start / Practice.** Add a secondary "Practice" affordance beside Start on the opening screen (`#startBlock`) → a scenario-picker modal → picking one enters practice mode (same conversation screen; Rule 1 keeps the base screen static). This is the natural onboarding moment and the front-and-center placement the assessment wants.

**Scenario data model** (small local record):
```
{ id, category, title, description,
  opensWith: 'partner' | 'user',   // who speaks first
  partnerPersona,                   // system-prompt text: who they are + their goal + tone
  register }                        // §8: sets the engine's register field explicitly
```

**Worldview-collection tie-in (note, not in the minimal build).** §8 observes the same machinery can run the AI *interviewing the user* to fill the worldview profile (a "scenario" whose partner is a friendly interviewer; answers → the worldview model). High-value follow-on; the minimal build is rehearsal scenarios only.

**Build sketch (when authorized):** a `practiceMode` flag + current scenario in `app.js`; `llm.generatePartnerUtterance`; a `playPartnerUtterance(text)` helper (speak → feed the normal generation path); the Start-Listening fork (`advancePracticePartner()` gated by `manualListenArmed`/`loadAutoRelisten`) and the post-selection fork; `tts.speak` voice-override + a partner-voice setting; a bundled `practice-scenarios.js` starter library; the pre-start Practice affordance + scenario-picker modal; and the `Practice: <scenario>` conversation stamp. **Status: DECIDED, not built.** Gate: valuable at alpha (onboarding/rehearsal) and a beta de-risking tool — a strong candidate to pull forward per the assessment.

---

## iPad / iOS port — build-ready spec written, NOT built; gated on a hardware probe (Ken, July 29 2026)

Ken asked for an architecture document for running Conversant AAC on an iPad — still served from GitHub Pages, still in a browser, retaining as much of the UI and the guidelines as possible. **Decisions (Ken, via AskUserQuestion):** (1) a **build-ready spec**, not a go/no-go memo — the decision to proceed is assumed; (2) **design the STT fallback in full** (paid cloud transcription as a first-class option), not merely flag the risk; (3) establish platform facts by **research + a capability probe run on Ken's real iPad**, not from recollection; (4) **straight to .docx** in house style. Plus a standing directive: **keep a running record of hardware requirements/limitations** (screen size, processor, I/O ports) encountered along the way — this became §8 of the document.

**Ken's Chrome/Edge assumption was corrected.** Ken noted that if Safari proves limited, "install Chrome or Edge on the iPad" would be a reasonable fallback. It is not: Apple requires every iOS/iPadOS browser to use WebKit, so Chrome/Edge/Firefox on iPad are Safari's engine in a different wrapper and inherit the same limits. (CLAUDE.md's existing iPad note already said this; the new finding is the nuance.) Apple's **BrowserEngineKit** does permit real alternative engines but **only in the EU**, on iOS 17.4+/iPadOS 18+ — unavailable to a US user. And the nuance runs the *wrong* way: capabilities can be granted per-app, and `SpeechRecognition` has historically been reachable only from Safari proper, so the third-party shells may be **worse** than Safari for the one capability that matters most. The probe tests all three so Ken can see it rather than take my word.

**Five findings that shaped the document** (all re-tested by the probe, because reported ≠ observed):
1. **The two flags Conversant is built on are precisely the two reported broken on iOS.** `webkitSpeechRecognition` exists (iOS 14.5+, requires Siri enabled), but `continuous = true` is reported broken (mic never stops, results never delivered) and `interimResults` is unreliable in WebKit. `stt.js:184-185` sets both. So this is not "speech is flaky on iOS" — it is a direct hit on Continuous Partner Capture. Interim results are load-bearing in **three** places: the live transcript (`stt.js:222`), interruption capture (`getCurrentTranscript`, `stt.js:310` — the v0.5.77 fix), and one of the echo filter's four matching strategies (`stt.js:169`).
2. **The adaptation is closer to the existing code than expected.** `stt.js:225-250` *already* restarts recognition on `onend` while `listeningIntent` holds, and already flushes the pending interim before restarting — the same shape as the documented iOS workaround (non-continuous + restart). What changes is the flag, the restart delay, a `visibilitychange` guard, and the `onerror` handling (`stt.js:252-260` clears `listeningIntent` on any surfaced error, which would tear down listening on iOS where sessions end constantly by design).
3. **Storage is a much smaller change than the "FSA doesn't exist on iOS" framing suggests.** OPFS uses the *same* `FileSystemDirectoryHandle`/`FileSystemFileHandle` interfaces as FSA, so once `dirHandle` points at the OPFS root, `readFile`/`writeFile`/`getSettingsDir`/`getConversationsDir`/`appendErrorFile` work **unchanged**. Only `pickDataFolder` and `restoreDataFolder` are rewritten. One fork the probe decides: if Safari offers only `createSyncAccessHandle` (worker-scoped) rather than `createWritable`, all writes must move into a Worker — a much larger change.
4. **A durability-vs-speech pincer.** Home Screen install is the classic answer for iOS storage durability, but speech recognition is reported to **fail silently in standalone mode** (feature detection passes, nothing happens). The likely escape is finding 3's companion: Safari 17+ `persist()` is a real eviction exemption, so a plain tab may be durable enough. **This is the single most important thing the probe settles** — hence running it in both modes.
5. **TTS has its own iOS rules.** `speak()` needs a genuine user-gesture unlock (Conversant fires placeholders on *timers*), `getVoices()` returns a truncated/empty list (so Practice Mode's distinct partner voice may be impossible — fall back to rate/pitch differentiation), and speech stops when backgrounded. Also `tts.js:47` does `cancel()` then `speak()` in the same tick, a known iOS trouble spot.

**Two concrete defects found in passing (fix whenever the iPad work starts, or sooner):** `app.js:201-202` tells a user whose browser lacks recognition to *"Use Chrome or Edge"* — wrong advice on an iPad, and it routes through `ui.setStatus`, which has been **visually hidden** since v0.5.2, so it is never seen anyway. And `styles.css:125` sets `height: 100vh` with `overflow: hidden`: in a Safari tab `100vh` is the *toolbars-retracted* height, so the **dock (Express Panel / keyboard) would sit under the toolbar and be untappable** — a hard failure of the primary interaction surface, fixed by `100dvh`.

**Layout finding, measured rather than assumed (this corrected my own first draft).** `styles.css:23-24` calibrates the proportional layout to a fleet of ~1280–1465 × 853–976 CSS px with `clamp()` bounds set outside it. Every iPad is outside that fleet, but running the real rule — `clamp(14px, 1.6vmin + 1.8px, 24px)` — against actual iPad dimensions shows only the **mini** clamps: iPad Pro 13" (1366×1024) → 18.18px, Pro 11" (1194×834) → 15.14px, Air/standard 11" (1180×820) → 14.92px, **mini (1133×744) → 13.70px clamped to 14.00px**. So the work is **widening the fleet definition and re-deriving the bounds**, not redesigning the layout; the iPad mini is the likely lower bound of support.

**Deliverables (committed locally, NOT pushed — a push deploys the app):**
- **`Conversant AAC iPad Architecture.docx`** — 12 sections, ~6,800 words, generated by **`generate-ipad-architecture-doc.js`** (docx-js, house style). Every non-obvious claim is marked **`[SOURCE]`** (read from the v0.5.99 codebase), **`[RESEARCH]`** (public docs, cited in §12), or **`[PROBE]`** (unresolved). Covers: purpose/scope (one codebase, capability-adaptive — explicitly not a fork), platform reality, storage, the three-tier speech design, speech out, UI/layout, install/updates/single-instance, **§8 hardware requirements and limitations** (the running record), build order, the lost/gained ledger, and open questions. Tracked in DOC-SYNC.
- **`ipad-probe.html`** — a self-contained capability probe. Verified clean in a desktop browser (no console errors; three of its own bugs found and fixed during verification — `dvh` support inferred from a measurement instead of feature-detected, duplicate report rows on re-run, and misleading rows at a zero viewport). **Not yet run on an iPad.**

**Three things worth carrying forward.** (a) **Export/Import is the real replacement for the portable data folder** — OPFS is invisible to the user, so the v0.2.25 "copy `worldview.json` between PCs" flow dies and backup/transfer/ownership all need building explicitly; this is also exactly the mechanism the long-planned **cross-device transfer** feature needs, so **building it on Windows first would de-risk it and deliver value even if the iPad port stalls**. (b) **If cloud STT ships, it does NOT break the no-backend principle** (user's own key, own device, own spend — same model as the Anthropic key), but it **doubles the hardest part of onboarding**, and because streaming bills per minute of audio while auto-resume keeps the mic open, the adapter **must gate the upstream on actual speech or the user pays for silence**. (c) **iPad brings genuine wins**: commodity keyguards and mounts (directly serving Spatial Stability), **Guided Access** (a better single-instance guarantee than the planned Web Locks design — SEC-9), cellular connectivity (the app needs the network for both AI *and* transcription), and mature OS-level Switch Control for the future scanning renderer. Apple **Personal Voice is not reachable from a web app** — the most significant thing the platform has that the web cannot touch.

**PROBE RUN — July 30 2026, iPad 10th generation, iPadOS 26, Safari 26.6. The results forced the architecture, and three of my [RESEARCH] claims were wrong.**

**The decisive finding — a confirmed pincer with no escape.** Speech recognition works in **exactly one** environment: **Safari, browser tab** (continuous: 22 interim + 1 final, 4274 ms to first result; non-continuous: 16 interim + 1 final, 1851 ms). It delivers **nothing** — starts, then aborts with no interim and no final — in a Home Screen app and in **both Chrome and Edge tabs**. Meanwhile `navigator.storage.persist()` is **GRANTED only in a Home Screen app** and **DENIED in every browser tab**, in all three browsers, **including with a bookmark in place** (tested explicitly — Safari's engagement heuristic does not rescue it). **And the two do not share storage:** persistence granted in the installed app, and two later tab runs on the same origin still reported "not persisted" — so a Home Screen app and a Safari tab are **separate storage silos**, which kills the obvious workaround (install once to earn persistence, then run in a tab) *and* means moving between modes loses all data unless the user exports first. Also measured: all three "Home Screen" runs report an **identical Safari UA**, so Add to Home Screen always produces a Safari shell whichever browser created it — four distinct environments, not six.

**FOUR [RESEARCH] claims the probe overturned** (the reason the probe was built before any code): (1) **`continuous` is NOT broken on iOS** — both modes worked in Safari's tab, so Tier 1 (free, built-in) is viable; the only caveat is that continuous measured **2.4 s slower to first word**, so the iPad adapter should default to non-continuous. (2) **`getVoices()` is NOT truncated** — **68 voices**, so Practice Mode's distinct partner voice works as designed. **[QUALIFIED July 31 2026 — the count is real but the roster is nearly all unusable; see "Apple voices on iPadOS" below. Two distinct voices are available, but the second one is a 1980s novelty voice.]** (3) **The `100vh` trap did NOT reproduce** in a Safari tab (0 px hidden); it appeared once in standalone (32 px). Use `100dvh` anyway — free and correct — but it was overstated as a guaranteed hard failure. (4) **`inputmode="none"` DOES suppress the iPadOS keyboard** (Ken, direct observation July 30 2026: the plain control field raised the keyboard in every scenario while both the `inputmode="none"` and `readonly` fields left it closed) — so **`keyboard.js` needs no iPad change at all** and the planned `readonly` workaround should NOT be built.

**APPLE VOICES ON iPadOS — MEASURED July 31 2026, iPad 10th gen, iPadOS 26.6, Safari 26.6, browser tab. Enhanced and Premium voices are NOT reachable from a web app, and downloading them changes nothing.** Ken installed **Ava, Ava (Enhanced) and Ava (Premium)** on the device (via **Settings → Accessibility → Live Speech → Preferred Voices**), fully quit and relaunched, and read the app's new voice diagnostic (Settings → About). **The list did not move: still exactly 68 voices, no Ava of any kind, and not one `enhanced` or `premium` voiceURI in the whole roster.**

**What the 68 actually are, and why this is a frozen roster rather than a reflection of what is installed:**
- **19 are 1980s-era novelty voices** — `com.apple.speech.synthesis.voice.{Albert,BadNews,Bahh,Bells,Boing,Bubbles,Cellos,Deranged,Fred,GoodNews,Hysterical,Junior,Kathy,Organ,Princess,Ralph,Trinoids,Whisper,Zarvox}`. **This is literally Ken's "the iPad voices are almost all silly"** — Bahh, Boing, Zarvox and Trinoids are gag voices, and they are 28% of the list.
- **45 are `com.apple.voice.super-compact.<lang>.<Name>`** — exactly **one voice per language** at the *smallest* quality tier (Daniel en-GB, Karen en-AU, Anna de-DE, …). `super-compact` is a tier BELOW `compact`; it was not previously known to us.
- **3 are `com.apple.voice.compact.<lang>`** (kn-IN, te-IN, bn-IN) and **1 is `com.apple.voice.compact.en-US.Samantha`** — the only ordinary en-US voice on offer.
- **The inference that matters: "one voice per language at the lowest quality, plus the legacy novelty set" is a curated, fixed list — not an enumeration of installed voices.** So this is not a caching or a download-location problem, and no further downloading will change it.
- **PLATFORM FACT worth keeping (Ken, July 31 2026): in iPadOS 26 the "Spoken Content" screen is renamed and moved to Settings → Accessibility → *Read & Speak*.** That is why it appeared to be missing. **The Ava voices downloaded through Live Speech are listed there**, which kills the obvious remaining hypothesis — the download is **system-wide, not scoped to Live Speech** — and confirms the voices really are installed on the device while remaining invisible to `getVoices()`. *(Record this location: it is where a user or supporter manages voices on iPadOS, and the old name is what every existing instruction says.)*
- **One variable left untested, expected to change nothing:** setting the *system* spoken-content voice under Read & Speak to Ava (Premium), then fully quitting and relaunching. Given a fixed roster this should not add a voice to `getVoices()`, but it is the last cheap check and would turn "very unlikely" into measured.
- **Also measured: every one of the 68 reports `default: true`**, so `voice.default` is useless as a discriminator on iPadOS. `pickPartnerVoice()` uses `voices.find(v => v.default)` to resolve "Browser default" to a real voice; it returns `voices[0]` = Samantha, which *is* the true system default — correct, but by ordering rather than by the flag. Do not lean harder on `default` without re-checking this.

**Two consequences that follow directly from the roster (both recorded as to-dos, NOT yet built):**
1. **Practice Mode's "Auto" partner voice will pick a novelty voice on iPad.** Auto deliberately chooses a voice that isn't the user's, preferring the same language; with Samantha taken, every remaining en-US option is a gag voice, so the rehearsal partner speaks as Albert or Zarvox. The fix is to exclude `com.apple.speech.synthesis.voice.*` from Auto's candidates (and probably to sort them last in the pickers) — filtering Apple's novelty voices is standard practice for web speech apps.
2. **The tier label collapses two real tiers into one.** `voiceQuality()` labels `super-compact` as "Compact" (the hyphen satisfies its word boundary), so Samantha (genuinely `compact`) and Daniel (`super-compact`) read identically while differing in quality. Ascending order is `super-compact < compact < enhanced < premium`.

**Strategic consequence — ACTED ON: Deepgram Aura TTS moved from "nice to have" to "the only route to a decent voice on iPad," and was built the same day (v0.5.99 dev, `44ae577`, confirmed speaking on the device).** The recorded follow-on (same key as the transcription work) was sequenced *after* transcription is proven on device; that sequencing still holds, but the justification is now much stronger — on iPad the built-in choice is Samantha or a joke voice, with no Apple upgrade path reachable from the web at any price. **Apple Personal Voice remains unreachable** for the separate, already-recorded reason.

> **On the meta-finding below, in fairness to the record: this is a case where the published reports were RIGHT.** "iOS 17 exposed some high-quality voices to web apps, gone again since" matches what we measured. The July-30 probe overturned four of eight researched claims, all in the pessimistic direction; this one went the other way. **The lesson is not "the record is always wrong" — it is "measure it," which cuts both ways.**

> **META-FINDING, and the most transferable thing here: eight [RESEARCH] claims were tested and FOUR were wrong — a 50% error rate, erring consistently in one direction (describing iOS as more broken than it is).** The published record of iOS/WebKit limitations is substantially out of date relative to iPadOS 26. **Treat every remaining `[RESEARCH]` claim in the iPad document as a hypothesis, not a fact** — where one would cost real work to accommodate, test it before designing around it. *Also worth carrying:* the keyboard question was settled by **Ken looking at the screen for twenty seconds** after the probe's viewport-based detection failed to answer it (its control measurement never completed, leaving three runs of clean-looking results that proved nothing). **When a check is cheap by eye and expensive to automate correctly, use the eye.**

**Other measured facts:** OPFS `createWritable` works on the main thread in all six runs → **the Worker rewrite fork is avoided**; quota 33,792 MB. `speak()` was **silent without a gesture in all six runs** → the unlock is mandatory. Usable viewport is **1180 × 763 in a tab / 1180 × 788 installed** against a nominal 820 — putting root type at **14.01 px / 14.41 px against a 14 px floor**, i.e. this iPad is *at* the clamp floor, so re-deriving the bounds is not optional. Safe-area bottom inset **25 px in a tab, 0 px installed**. `screen.orientation.lock` **absent**; Vibration unsupported; Web Locks, Wake Lock, Clipboard, and service workers all supported. Mic granted everywhere at 48 kHz with **echoCancellation already on by default**.

**DECISION (Ken, July 30 2026): ship BOTH iPad modes and let the user choose.** *Conversation mode* (Safari tab) = free built-in speech, evictable storage. *Protected mode* (Home Screen) = durable storage, partner capture requires a paid transcription key — and **without one it is a legitimate configuration, not a broken one**: the app degrades to manual AAC (Express Panel + "In my own words") with durable data, exactly the AI-optional property already established. **I flagged that shipping both creates a mode-switch data-loss trap** (separate silos) **and Ken chose it anyway**; the design therefore neutralizes the trap rather than re-arguing it — **Export/Import becomes the bridge**, mode switching routes through a guided export-then-import flow, and in Conversation mode **backup must be AUTOMATIC** (eviction after 7 days of non-use describes an illness or hospital stay — precisely when the user has not prepared). **Build-order consequence: Export/Import is promoted to Phase 1, ahead of everything, and should be built on Windows first** where it is testable and delivers cross-device transfer value even if the iPad work stalls. Phase 6 (paid streaming transcription) is **severable** — Conversation mode is a complete product without it.

**Build identification — Settings → About is the permanent home; the Start-screen stamp is temporary (Ken, July 30 2026).** The deploy workflow substitutes the commit sha into `@@BUILD@@` in `app.js` and `sw.js` (the trial repo has no workflow, so `scripts/`/the publish step does it there). **Settings → About** now reads `Version 0.5.99 · 9e73383 — Phase 1 Proof of Concept` — the release number with the build appended, which is what a bug report needs, since several deploys share one version during a dev cycle. **`#buildStamp` under the Start button is the temporary copy**, and exists only because a start-up failure makes Settings unreachable — exactly when you most need to know which build you have. **Ken's condition for removing it: once the iPad Home Screen app reliably gets past Start.** Removal = `#buildStamp` in `index.html`, the stamping block at the foot of `app.js`, and its CSS rule; the `BUILD_ID` constant and the About line stay.

**Paid speech-to-text — Deepgram, chosen because it is the only cheap one that works WITHOUT A SERVER (Ken authorized the build, July 30 2026).** Ken confirmed on the device that STT does not work in the iPad Home Screen app, and asked for a service. **Deepgram is NOT the cheapest — AssemblyAI is ~3× cheaper ($0.15/hr vs $0.46/hr) — and was rejected anyway**, because its streaming token must be minted **server-to-server**, so using it would require running a backend. That is the one architectural line this project cannot cross: "no server infrastructure the project must pay for" is the reason it can outlast the funded AAC projects that were shelved. Deepgram documents browser-direct connection (the key travels in the `Sec-WebSocket-Protocol` handshake), which fits the existing user-brings-their-own-key model exactly. **Verified live from a browser**: a bogus key is reported rejected through a real handshake, so the architectural bet is confirmed, not assumed. Pricing: **$0.0077/min Nova-3 streaming (~$0.46/hr), billed per second, $200 free credit (~430 hrs) on signup, no card**.
> **The cost model shapes the design, and this is the load-bearing part: billing is per second of audio SUBMITTED, not per second connected.** The app holds the mic open for the whole conversation (auto-resume restarts it after every exchange), so streaming continuously would bill an hour for a visit containing twelve minutes of speech. **`vad.js`** is a pure, unit-tested gate (hysteresis so a voice at the threshold can't chatter the upstream; **hang time** so gaps between words don't chop an utterance; **pre-roll** so the syllable that opened the gate isn't lost) and the socket stays open with free `KeepAlive` while audio is uploaded only during speech. A simulated 10-minute session with ~30s of speech bills <60s — that test is the feature's justification, not decoration. **This is also why raw linear16 PCM is sent rather than `MediaRecorder`:** webm/opus is a *stream* whose header is only in the first chunk, so a gated middle chunk is undecodable; PCM frames are stateless and can be gated, buffered and replayed.
> **Architecture:** the shared core stays in `stt.js` — accumulation, the TTS-echo filter, the silence checkpoint, `dropLastStatement` — and a backend supplies **text only**. The user's silence period is a *setting* and "Ask them to repeat" is a *feature*; neither may behave differently because the audio came from Deepgram. `stt.init({source})` selects; `stt-deepgram.js` is the second backend; the built-in recognizer remains the default and the free path. The paid backend also **bypasses the platform verdict** (it does its own capture, so "Safari delivers nothing in a Home Screen app" doesn't apply). `deepgramKey` is in `PROFILE_EXCLUDE` alongside `apiKey` (SEC-6 — never into a cloud-synced folder or a backup). Usage is counted in seconds (`usageSttSeconds`) because, unlike the AI calls, transcription bills while nobody is doing anything deliberate. **Not yet done:** thresholds in `vad.js` are untuned guesses that need a real room; `ScriptProcessorNode` is used rather than `AudioWorklet` (no extra file, no `blob:` for the planned CSP) and should be revisited if audio glitches or the CSP lands; changing provider/key needs a reload.
> **BUILT AND CONFIRMED ON DEVICE (July 31 2026, build `44ae577`, iPadOS 26.6 Safari): the Test button speaks.** That single tap settles the three things that could not be checked from a desktop, and the first is the load-bearing one: **Deepgram accepts `Sec-WebSocket-Protocol` subprotocol auth on `/v1/speak`, so Aura is reachable browser-direct with no server** — the same architectural bet as the transcription backend, now measured rather than inferred. It also confirms the voice id format is right and that iOS will play the audio at all. **The no-server principle therefore holds for BOTH paid services.** *Still unconfirmed, and cheap to discover in use: the fifteen voice ids other than the tested one are derived from Deepgram's documented `[model]-[voice]-[language]` format rather than quoted, so a wrong one fails at its own Test button (not mid-conversation); real-conversation latency on the first utterance; and the browser-voice fallback against an actual network failure.* The concerns weighed below were real and each is addressed in the build — latency and per-character billing by the repeated-phrase cache, the timer/gesture problem by unlocking audio on Start, and the offline story by falling back to the device voice on any failure. **The original note follows as the record of what was weighed:**
>
> **FOLLOW-ON, recorded not built — Deepgram Aura TTS on the same key (Ken, July 30 2026): "the iPad voices are almost all silly."** True and appealing: one key, no new signup, and far better voices than the iOS built-ins — which also revisits the long-standing finding that browser TTS gives no prosody control (SSML is ignored by the shipped voices; see the *Shaping-Speech-Through-Text* note). **But it is a bigger change than it looks**, and should be judged on its own: every spoken path goes through it (placeholders fire on *timers*, and network TTS adds latency exactly where the app is fighting the 4-second silence threshold), it breaks the offline/degraded story (`speechSynthesis` needs no network), it needs an audio-playback path plus caching for repeated phrases, and it is billed per character on top of the transcription. Sequence it AFTER the transcription work is proven on the device.

**SUPERSEDED Aug 1 2026 — there is now ONE deployment. See the consolidation record below.** *(The July 30 decision this replaces: "the iPad trial is a TRIAL — one codebase on `main`, published to both," with `scripts/publish-ipad-trial.mjs` copying `app/` to a second Pages site at `/conversant-aac-ipad/`, and `namespace.js` giving that path an `ipad:` storage prefix so the two could not share data.)*

**DECISION (Ken, August 1 2026): collapse the two deployments into one — everything is served from `/conversant-aac/`.** Ken: *"we should be serving the files from /conversant-aac/ in all cases… we should be pushing the code to the same single URL as well. Any GitHub and local git separation should also be removed."*

**The reason that settles it: the separation was never real.** `publish-ipad-trial.mjs` reset a cached clone to `origin/main` and copied `app/` wholesale, so the trial site could only ever serve exactly what production served — confirmed by hashing five files from both live URLs on Aug 1 2026, all identical. There has never been an iPad build distinct from the Windows build, so the second URL could not have isolated an experiment even if one had been wanted. What it actually provided was a storage namespace, protecting against an experimental build corrupting tester data — a build the script structurally could not publish. Against that it cost a second repo, a publish script with four guards, a cached clone, an extra release step, an ordering trap that bit on the day it was written, and the July 30 drift incident that cost an afternoon diagnosing a build Ken had never been running. `namespace.js`'s own header had already named the exit condition — *"once iPad support merges into the single shipping app there is only one deployment and nothing to separate; at that point delete TRIAL_PATHS and this whole module folds away"* — and Ken's decision that **iPad is a supported platform** is what made that condition true. The documents said "supported" while the infrastructure said "trial."

**Removed:** `app/js/namespace.js` and its six importers (`storage`, `worldview`, `relationships`, `control-phrases`, `express-panel`, `prediction`), `tests/namespace.test.mjs`, `scripts/publish-ipad-trial.mjs`, the cached clone, the service-worker precache entry, and release-ritual step 7. **Production was always unprefixed, so this is a no-op for every existing tester** — verified in the running app: after the removal a settings write still produces the key `aac_settings`, with no prefixed key anywhere. The `Volksswitch/conversant-aac-ipad` repo is Ken's to delete (the local `gh` token deliberately lacks `delete_repo`).

**Migration cost, and why it was nil here:** iPad data lived under the `ipad:` prefix and in an `ipad` OPFS subdirectory, so at the new URL the app would find an empty profile. The remedy is Export before, Import after — the bridge already built for moving between the two iPad modes. Ken confirmed he had no iPad data worth keeping, so no migration was performed. **If a second deployment is ever wanted again** — a staging channel serving code production does *not* have, which is a genuinely different thing — `namespace.js` is recoverable from git history.

**What still holds from July 30, and is load-bearing:** **`platform.js`** remains the seam for iOS-only behavior, and every iOS decision must resolve to the previous Windows value on desktop (`speechConfig()` → continuous, no restart delay, no visibility guard; `speechRecognitionSupport()` → usable, so the listening notice never appears; `--safe-bottom` → 0; `100dvh` ≡ `100vh`; the OPFS backend reached only where there is no folder picker). **Working rule: iOS-only behavior goes behind a capability check or a CSS value that is zero elsewhere — never a fork or a build flag. If a change CANNOT be made invisible, that is the signal it is a shared change and must be judged on its merits for Windows too.** The residual risk is not in the capability-gated code but in shared **layout and timing** changes, so the desktop path stays verified in the preview for every change. Ken's original framing still governs: *"I want common fixes to be applied to both the windows and iOS apps."*

**DECISION (Ken, July 30 2026): the capability verdict WARNS, it never BLOCKS — "give Safari every opportunity to surprise us."** The first cut disabled the Listen button wherever `platform.js` reported capture unusable (the iPad Home Screen app and the Chrome/Edge shells). Ken reversed it: **"for now, don't disable the listening function… I may have misread the test results but when running the probe, it appeared that the placeholder statements wouldn't fire and I was positively surprised when they did."** That precedent is the argument — a measurement taken on one build of one iPadOS is *evidence*, not a permanent property, and a button that refuses to try can never discover that Apple fixed something. **The two questions are therefore kept separate in `speechRecognitionSupport()`, and callers must keep them separate: `apiPresent`** (is there a recognizer to call at all? — false is the only true dead end, and the only case that still disables the button) **vs. `usable`** (is it *measured* to deliver results here? — false warns, then allows the attempt). Consequences: **`stt.init()` now runs wherever the API is present**, because a live button with no initialized recognizer would be the silent no-op the notice exists to prevent; the notice's wording moved from "Listening does not work…" to "**may** not work… **Try it** — if nothing appears when the other person speaks, open Conversant in Safari instead"; and Practice Mode keeps its exemption even in the no-recognizer case (that button cues the AI partner and never opens a mic). **The known cost, accepted:** on a platform where recognition genuinely delivers nothing, pressing Listen lights the mic and hears nothing forever — exactly the failure the notice was built to prevent — so the warning must stay honest and prominent. *(Recorded but NOT built: auto-suppressing the notice once capture is observed to actually deliver a result on this device, so a surprise stops the app from crying wolf. Cheap; deferred because Ken is mid-experiment and may want the warning to keep appearing.)* Unit-tested in `tests/platform.test.mjs`, which also guards the inverted-detection trap (iPadOS Safari's UA says "Macintosh" and not "iPad", so a naive `/iPad/` test misses the one browser that works and matches the two that don't).

**Pre-start sequencing extended to four steps (Ken, July 30 2026): Start → "What's new" → listening notice → API-key notice → conversation.** The listening notice was being raised at load, alongside the Start button and the upgrade screen — which violates the v0.5.96 rule that pre-start screens **never overlap**. It is now step 3 of that chain, shown on its own, dismissed with "Continue anyway". It sits **before** the API-key notice because it is about *where the user opened the app* and its remedy ("open it in Safari instead") is something they may want to act on before anything else, whereas an API key can be added from Settings at any time. All three intermediate screens remain optional and are skipped when not needed.

**Still open (§11), now five items:** whether the TTS gesture unlock persists for a whole session (if not, placeholders can't be spoken on iPad); iPad mini support (it computes *below* the clamp floor); whether accumulated engagement eventually earns a tab persistence grant (worth re-testing after weeks of real use, but **do not design around it**); cellular data cost of streaming transcription; and whether iPad becomes the recommended platform or stays an alternative (the doc assumes alternative).

**Probe hosting — DONE AND CLEANED UP.** Was hosted July 29–30 2026 at `https://volksswitch.github.io/conversant-aac-ipad-probe/` (public repo `Volksswitch/conversant-aac-ipad-probe`, Pages from `main` root), deliberately **separate from this repo** so it never touched Conversant's release train — a push to this project's `main` deploys the app. **Ken deleted the repo via the GitHub web UI on July 30 2026 once the results were in; verified gone (API 404, Pages 404).** Claude could not delete it — the local `gh` token carries `gist`/`read:org`/`repo`/`workflow` but **not `delete_repo`**; the browser route was preferred over `gh auth refresh -s delete_repo` specifically to avoid permanently leaving a delete-capable credential on the machine for a one-off cleanup.

> **The probe deliberately shared the app's origin (`volksswitch.github.io`), and the reasoning is worth keeping for any future probe.** `navigator.storage.persist()` grants can depend on site engagement, so a brand-new throwaway origin could be *denied* persistence where the app's own established origin would be *granted* — testing on a different origin risked a **false negative on the single most important storage question**. (The measured result — tab DENIED even when bookmarked, standalone GRANTED — is therefore trustworthy in a way an isolated-origin test would not have been.) Side effects, all now moot: it granted persistent storage to the app's own origin (harmless); it created and deleted `probe.json` + a `conversations/` directory in that origin's OPFS (no collision — the Windows app uses FSA, not OPFS); and it added one page to the shared origin **SEC-1** flags, mitigated at the time by the probe having no XSS sink (every dynamic value went through `textContent`, never `innerHTML`) and **fully resolved by the deletion**.

**The probe source survives at [`ipad-probe.html`](ipad-probe.html) in this repo** (committed `f9c5dc5`; verified byte-identical to the deployed copy, SHA-256 `78AB1F94…`, before deletion). Re-hosting it is a copy-to-a-new-Pages-repo away if a later iPadOS release needs re-testing — which the 50% research-error-rate finding above suggests is worth doing rather than trusting the record.

---

## Platform naming, the paid-service journey, and document structure (Ken, August 1 2026)

Ken settled the vocabulary for talking about the two configurations, the shape of the product documents, and — across four corrections in one conversation — the sequencing of the paid services. The sequencing is the part with the most downstream consequence, so it is stated first.

### The paid-service journey — free to trial, paid once convinced

**Deepgram is an UPGRADE, not the default (Ken's correction, and the load-bearing one).** An earlier draft of this record had a Deepgram key as the normal path for nearly every user, on the strength of voice quality. Ken: *"Users will be 'trialing' the app at first and won't want to incur a second account/charge until they are convinced of the value of the app."* So the shipped defaults are right as they stand — this device's voices, this browser's own hearing — and **every document must present the free configuration as the starting point**, with the paid services as something you move to deliberately.

**But the destination genuinely is paid, for the voice, on BOTH platforms (Ken).** *"PC and iPad users will likely be paying for Deepgram for the voice."* The draw is voice quality and identity — and it grows as the user set expands, because **younger voices** are not something the built-in voices offer at all. This is not an iPad workaround; it is where a satisfied user of either platform ends up. **iPad users in Home Screen mode ALSO pay for STT** — that one is platform-specific, because the probe measured that recognition delivers nothing there.

**Browser STT must stay first-class on Chromium (Ken).** It is reliable there and it keeps the bill down, so the valuable steady-state Chromium configuration is **free browser hearing + a paid Deepgram voice**. Note this is exactly the pairing that was unreachable until August 1 2026, because the Deepgram key field was nested inside the *transcription* choice and vanished if you chose free hearing — fixed by hoisting the key to the top of the new Speech tab.

**The three cost configurations, which the documents should state plainly:**

| Configuration | Hearing | Speaking | What it costs beyond the AI |
|---|---|---|---|
| Chromium (Windows / Chromebook / Mac) | browser, free | device voice, free → Deepgram voice | nothing → ~3¢ per 1,000 characters spoken |
| iPad, Safari tab ("Conversation mode") | browser, free | device voice, free → Deepgram voice | nothing → ~3¢ per 1,000 characters spoken |
| iPad, Home Screen app ("Protected mode") | **Deepgram, required** | device voice, free → Deepgram voice | ~46¢ per hour of speech, + the voice if chosen |

**The two iPad modes are a JOURNEY, not a preference — this is the synthesis the documents should carry.** A trialing iPad user starts in the **Safari tab**, precisely because it needs no second account. The **Home Screen app** is where they graduate once convinced: better use of the screen, durable storage — and by then they are likely paying for the voice anyway, so adding transcription to the same key is an increment rather than a new barrier. **The consequence is that Export/Import is not an edge case but the standard iPad graduation path**, because the two modes are separate storage silos (measured July 30 2026: persistence granted in the installed app was invisible to two later tab runs on the same origin). The guided export-then-import flow must be built and documented as the normal way to move up, not as a recovery tool.

**Screen real estate pushes BOTH platforms toward the installed app (Ken, August 1 2026).** *"the vast majority of users will want a desktop app implementation so that the app best utilizes screen real-estate."* Consistent with UI Layout Rule 2 — vertical space is the precious resource, and browser chrome spends it. **Honest caveat to carry into the iPad material: the measured gain on iPad is modest** — the probe recorded a 763 px usable viewport in a tab against 788 px installed, plus a 25 px bottom safe-area inset the tab has and the installed app does not, so roughly 25–50 px, call it 3–7% of height. On Chromium the gain is larger and costs nothing, so **install is an unambiguous win there and a real trade on iPad** (screen and durability against a paid transcription requirement). Do not flatten that into "installed is better."

### Naming — Chromium vs. WebKit, but only where the reader is an engineer

**The engineering axis is the browser ENGINE: `Chromium` vs. `WebKit`** (capital K). Ken proposed it; it is the right axis because it is the actual causal variable and because it makes the one error this project has already made impossible to state — "install Chrome on the iPad" is a contradiction under it, since Chrome on iPad *is* WebKit (and measured *worse* than Safari: recognition delivers nothing in Chrome or Edge there). Two things device-based naming gets wrong that this gets right: the flagship Chromium device is a **Surface tablet**, not a laptop, so "laptop" was never the right word for that bucket; and **a Mac running Safari is a WebKit configuration on a laptop**, which no device-based split can express. *(Open, and worth measuring rather than asserting — the probe's 50% research-error rate applies to me too: whether Safari on macOS supports `showDirectoryPicker()`. If it does not, Mac + Safari behaves like the iPad for storage, and the Product Overview's "Mac" support claim quietly assumes Chrome or Edge.)*

**Below that axis sit the two iPad modes, already named:** **Conversation mode** (Safari tab) and **Protected mode** (Home Screen app). These are a level *under* WebKit, not an alternative to it — and on iPad they matter more than the engine does, since they differ on the two things that decide everything (speech works in one, storage persists in the other). *(Minor flag, Ken's coinage so his call: "Protected mode" reads as the safe one for conversations when it is the one that cannot hear anyone without a paid key.)*

**The vocabulary is TWO-LAYER, and this is a rule, not a preference (Ken): engine names belong only where the reader is an engineer.** Ken: *"terms like Chromium and WebKit make sense in an architecture document but not in a product overview or user manual. Those documents have different readers. Users won't know what those terms mean and won't want to understand them. Instead, use terms that may not be as concise but are recognizable."*
- **Architecture Overview, CLAUDE.md, code comments** → `Chromium` / `WebKit`.
- **Product Overview, User Manuals** → recognizable device names — **"Windows & Chromebook"** / **"iPad"** — and plain descriptions of the modes ("in the Safari browser" / "installed on your Home Screen"). Longer is fine; unexplained jargon is not. `Gecko`/Firefox is neither engine, so where the pair might read as exhaustive, write "Chromium (the supported configuration)".

### Document structure

**The rule underneath all of it: unify by what the product IS, split by what the reader must DO.**

- **Architecture Overview — MERGE the iPad document into it.** One codebase with capability-gated seams was a deliberate decision (*"explicitly not a fork"*), and a separate iPad architecture document is a structural claim that there are two architectures; documents shape thinking, and two of them will drift into describing two systems. The real divergence is a handful of seams — `platform.js`, storage backend, STT backend, TTS backend, install/update, layout clamp bounds — which is a set of *sections*, not a document. Target shape: platform notes **inline at each seam**, plus one **Platform Configurations** section defining the vocabulary once, plus the hardware record as an appendix. **What does NOT merge: the `[MEASURED]`/`[RESEARCH]`/`[PROBE]` evidence apparatus** — that is a probe report, not architecture. Extract it to a short standing **Platform Measurements** record, which is where it belongs anyway and keeps its value when a future iPadOS needs re-testing.
- **Product Overview — UNIFY.** It is entirely "what the product is," which is identical on both platforms; the differences are a few sentences about where data lives and what it costs. Two of them would be 95% identical, would drift, and would imply two products.
- **User Manuals — TWO, each SELF-CONTAINED (Ken, overruling my cheaper proposal, and he is right about the readers).** I proposed one shared manual plus a short per-platform supplement to avoid duplicating the large shared content. Ken: *"humans MUCH prefer a single document rather than trying to manage two so self-contained is the way to go."* So the duplication cost is accepted deliberately — an iPad reader should never encounter a Windows instruction, and should never be asked to hold two documents open. **Standing consequence for "sync docs": a change to shared behavior must be applied to BOTH manuals in the same pass**, and a sync that touches one and not the other is incomplete.
- **The iPad material must include a TABLE of browser-tab vs. Home-Screen-app differences AND their implications (Ken).** Implications, not just facts — what each row means for the reader's decision. It belongs in the iPad User Manual (leading the mode decision, since it is the first thing that reader must choose) and in the merged Architecture Overview's Platform Configurations section.

### iPad is a SUPPORTED PLATFORM in the documents, and it carries release numbers (Ken, August 1 2026)

**Every document treats iPad as first-class alongside Chromium — not as an experiment, a trial, or a hedge.** This is a change of register from the July 30 record, which called it "the iPad trial." **That word was about the ENGINEERING arrangement and it still holds** — one codebase on `main` with capability-gated seams — but it must not leak into the product documents. A reader deciding whether an iPad will work for a non-speaking family member should not be told they are joining an experiment. So: no "experimental", no "trial", no "we are investigating whether" — state what the platform does and what it costs, in the same voice used for Windows.

**ONE release number covers both platforms, which is already true and should stay that way (Ken, correcting an overstatement of mine).** There is nothing to implement: it is one codebase and, since August 1 2026, one deployment, so there is a single `APP_VERSION` and a single build id by construction. **Do not introduce a separate iPad release number.** Documents should therefore quote one version for the product, not one per platform.

**One consequence that needs Ken's call, flagged not acted on: the `## Held back — iPad work in progress, not announced` section of `CHANGELOG.md` is now at odds with treating iPad as supported.** It exists because iPad pushes reach Windows users and the work was not to be announced. **Decide at the next `bump Conversant`** whether to move those entries under `## Unreleased` and regenerate. Two of them (**Export/Import** and the **API-key Paste button**) were already flagged as Windows-relevant and due for announcement regardless.

*(This flagged the second Pages site as still being named a trial and advised against renaming anything on the strength of it. **Ken went further the same day and removed the second site altogether** — see the consolidation record above. The data-migration tail it warned about was real but empty in practice: Ken had no iPad data to carry across.)*

---

## Further Design Thoughts (June 15 2026) — recorded, not built

A batch of Ken's thoughts captured this session. **Status: recorded only — none are implemented.** Items tagged **[→ Overview To Do]** also need to land in the Architecture and/or Product Overview docs; those edits are deferred and listed in the **Overview-Document To Do List** section below (Ken: don't spend editing time on the Overview docs for a couple of thoughts — let them accumulate and batch).

1. **Multi-party conversations — future feature.** Today the model assumes one communication partner. Supporting more than one partner in a conversation (knowing who said what, who a response is directed to, turn-taking among several people) is a future capability, not Phase 1/2. Touches STT (speaker diarization), the transcript UI, partner recognition (Phase 2), and the sequence-stack engine. **[→ Overview To Do]**

2. **Conversational honesty — the user is a person, not an encyclopedia (design principle).** The system must make it natural for the user to say **"I don't know,"** **"I think X but I could be wrong,"** or to state a sincere belief that happens to be wrong. It must **not** turn the user into a know-it-all / information oracle / smart speaker that others can interrogate or exploit. Rationale: the system speaks *as* the user, not *for* them — it mirrors the user's actual knowledge state and fallibility, it does not optimize for being maximally helpful/correct like an assistant. Concrete implications: the move palette should be able to surface "I don't know" and hedged/uncertain framings (good fit with the existing **DISPREFERRED = hedge + account** slot); the generation system prompt must steer toward authentic, fallible self-expression and away from assistant-style omniscience. This is a guardrail against the AAC user being treated like Google. **[→ Overview To Do]**

   **2a. No fabricated autobiography — the fabrication corollary of honesty (Ken, June 20 2026; partial fix shipped v0.3.18).** Distinct, equally critical failure Ken caught live: with Tyler in the relationship graph and "I play online games with Tyler and friends most evenings" in the profile, the partner asks "what have you been up to?" and the AI invents a **specific episode** — "I played a game with Tyler last night and beat him!" The model treated the user as a fictional character and fantasized events in his life. Fix shipped in v0.3.18 (prompt rule in `llm.js`: standing/habitual profile facts are fair game, *specific episodes/outcomes/dates/scores not given* are forbidden; keep answers general — "been gaming with friends lately" — when a specific is unknown; DISPREFERRED account can't invent a concrete excuse either). **Decisions (Ken, June 20 2026):**
   - **Prompt-only, no verification pass.** Ken's reasoning (correct, and worth keeping): a second-pass verifier has **no more ground truth than the generator** — both know only the standing profile, neither knows whether the user actually beat Tyler last night. Verification could at best catch episodes that *contradict* known facts; it can never *confirm* an unstated episode is true. So autobiography is unverifiable without the autobiography, and the prompt rule is the right primary (and only) defense for now. Probabilistic, accepted.
   - **The real test comes with episodic-event support.** "Keep it general" is correct *only because the system currently has no record of actual events.* When we add a way for the user to **record real events in their life** (an episodic/journal store — the natural complement to the standing worldview profile, and a relative of the Phase-3 user-authored "my views" RAG documents in thought #7), the AI will have true episodes to draw on, and the honest test becomes: does it use the *recorded* events correctly rather than inventing ones? Revisit the "general only" stance then. **[→ Overview To Do — pairs with the conversational-honesty entry]**

3. **"What this system is NOT" — add an explicit section (content below).** The system is **not** a personal assistant and has no intention of becoming one. It does not: review or triage your email, make appointments or manage your calendar, take notes in class, prepare study materials / sample tests, do research tasks, or otherwise act as a productivity agent. It is a **conversation** tool — it helps a non-speaking person hold real-time, interactional conversation *as themselves*. Scoping this explicitly protects the product's focus and sets partner/supporter expectations (it is not a smart speaker, not Siri/Alexa, not an LLM chatbot bolted to a tablet). **[→ Overview To Do — this is primarily a Product Overview positioning section]**

4. **"Start Listening" must be a deliberate, per-session user action — never automatic at startup. BUILT (June 15 2026), v0.2.32.** *Implementation:* `app.js` `manualListenArmed` session flag — set `true` by a manual start in `toggleListening`, set `false` by a manual stop; the automatic stop when a response is selected does **not** clear it. `handleResponseSelected` auto-resumes only when `manualListenArmed && storage.loadAutoRelisten()`. Startup was already clean (`handleStart()` never auto-listens). APP_VERSION/CACHE_VERSION → `0.2.32`. Rules satisfied:
   - Start Listening **never** takes effect simply because the app launched/started. Microphone capture begins only when the user explicitly invokes Start Listening.
   - The user must invoke Start Listening **at least once during a session** before any automatic listening can occur.
   - **Auto-resume** (the existing `auto-resume-listening` setting) is gated behind that first manual invocation: it only resumes listening after a spoken response *if* the user has manually started listening at least once this session.
   - If the user **turns listening off** during a session, the gate resets — auto-resume will not fire again until the user manually invokes Start Listening once more.
   **[→ Overview To Do — privacy/consent behavior belongs in both overviews]**

5. **Recording indicator for the communication partner (privacy — open question, my recommendation: yes).** Question Ken raised: do we need a visible UI/hardware indication that recording is taking place, primarily for the partner's benefit? **My take: yes, recommended and arguably ethically/legally prudent.** Points: (a) the partner is "unaware of system internals" by design, but a mic-active indicator reveals *no* internals — it only signals capture, which is a different thing; (b) audio-capture consent varies by jurisdiction (one-party vs. two-party/all-party consent recording laws), so a clear "listening" indicator is good practice; (c) **we don't store partner audio** — STT transcribes and the audio is discarded — but the *transcript* is stored, so "listening" is the honest signal; (d) on a Surface tablet we control only on-screen UI, so the near-term answer is a **prominent on-screen recording indicator**; a hardware capture LED (like a webcam light) is the gold standard but only relevant to a future dedicated-hardware / AR-glasses form factor. Pairs with item 4 (deliberate Start Listening) — together they make capture visible and intentional. **[→ Overview To Do]**

6. **Age 16+ target — rationale + an appropriateness filter (open question, my recommendation below).** Ken isn't certain why he set 16+; candidate reasons: (a) scarcity of free youth/preteen TTS voices; (b) concern the AI might suggest responses inappropriate for under-16s. On the filter: **yes, an appropriateness filter is achievable.** Layered options, cheapest first: (1) a **system-prompt register instruction** in `buildProfileBlock()` (set the appropriateness band); (2) an explicit content guard on generated options; (3) provider-side safety settings. **Where to set it so it can't be trivially flipped:** Ken's instinct (a password/PIN-protected Settings area) is right — it fits the existing **Setup-tier, supporter-assisted** settings concept. **Important framing:** the system speaks *as the user*; an adult user gets to choose their own register (even a coarse one) — it's their voice. So the appropriateness filter is really for **minors and supporter-managed accounts**, not a global nanny on every user. Design it as an **optional, supporter-locked profile setting** (default off for adult self-managed use; on/locked for minor accounts), not an always-on filter. **[→ Overview To Do]**
   - **PARTIALLY DECIDED August 3 2026 (Ken) — vulgarity specifically: NO for now, and the eventual control is PER-PARTNER, not a global band.** Triggered live: a card reading *"Honestly? I think it's pretty solid. I fw it."* was offered as the PREFERRED response to a **17-year-old** ("fw" = "fuck with"). Ken: *"vulgarity should only be explicitly chosen and be a function of who the partner is — meaning an identified partner with this property set. That should be a future feature since we may need to gate it based on guardian approval and I have no idea how that will work. For now, the model should be told no vulgarity."*
     - **Two axes, and they must not be conflated.** *Speakability* (can a synthesizer say it?) is separate and already fixed — "fw" is unsayable whatever the register. *Register/vulgarity* is this decision, and it is untouched by that fix: **"I fucking love it" is perfectly sayable** and would still have been offered.
     - **The eventual shape is a property of a PERSON in the relationship graph, not a global setting** — the natural home is `attrs` on the `me→person` edge, alongside the standing relationship goal. That is a better fit than a profile-wide band: how coarse someone is with their brother and with their consultant are different questions, and a single switch cannot express it. **Blocked on guardian approval**, whose mechanism is genuinely unknown — do not design around a guess.
     - **BUILT NOW: `NO_VULGARITY` in `llm.js`**, applied to every generation prompt that produces speech — responses, lead statements, both repair paths, **and the Practice partner** (*my scoping call, easily reversed: there is no argument for the rehearsal partner being coarser than the user, and it speaks to the same person*). **Deliberately NOT applied to the transcript-cleanup prompt** — that processes what the PARTNER actually said, and censoring it there would corrupt the record.
     - **The load-bearing half is the refusal to INFER, not the prohibition.** The offending turn had **`partner: null`** and an **age in the profile**, and the model read both as licence. **An age with no instruction attached is worse than no age at all**: absent guidance, "17" is a cue for how to *sound*. So the rule names every signal that must not be read as permission — age, profile, how crude the partner sounds, informality of setting, and **the absence of any instruction to the contrary**. When the per-partner control is built, that clause is what must be narrowed rather than deleted.
     - **The asymmetry that justifies "no" as the default** (same shape as the `looksLikeClosing` precision trade): a card can be tapped by mistake — this population taps imprecisely, which is why the double-tap safeguard exists — and an obscenity spoken **in the user's own voice** to a support worker or a stranger cannot be taken back. A blander card can.
     - Verified against the live model with a 17-year-old profile and a deliberately crude partner turn ("that referee was absolute garbage, what the hell…"): all four responses clean, and the register still natural rather than prim. Guarded by a prompt-content test.

7. **Around RAG (Phase 3), accept user-authored documents representing the user's views.** When RAG support lands, let the user feed in documents (essays, prior writing, position statements, faith/values statements, etc.) that capture their views on various topics. These become retrievable worldview context — a richer, user-authored complement to the structured questionnaire. Fits the worldview/RAG progression already recorded (heuristic → client-side embeddings → vector store) and Phase 3's worldview shaping. **[→ Overview To Do — worldview/RAG section]**

8. **System requirements — be able to recommend CPU/disk specs; understand what onboard AI adds (my analysis).** Two distinct regimes:
   - **Current cloud architecture (Claude/other API):** requirements are **modest** — any modern Windows tablet running current Edge/Chrome, a few hundred MB for the cached app + local data folder, and a network connection. We should publish a simple recommended-spec sheet for this now (CPU/RAM are not the bottleneck; network reliability + a decent mic are).
   - **Onboard / local AI (running the LLM on-device):** this is a **major architectural fork**, not a setting. It introduces: a capable **GPU or NPU** with substantial **VRAM** (~8–24 GB depending on model/quantization), **16–32 GB+ system RAM**, **multi-GB model files on disk** (~4–40 GB each), plus **thermal/battery** load on a tablet — and, at tablet-feasible model sizes, **noticeably weaker quality** than frontier cloud models. It also strains the current "static web app, no backend, runs in a browser" design: in-browser local inference means **WebGPU (WebLLM/MLC)**, which is heavy and limited. Recommendation: treat onboard AI as a **separate future track with its own (much higher) spec sheet**; keep cloud as the baseline. **[→ Overview To Do — system-requirements section; onboard AI as a future track]**

9. **Future: support near-real-time and batched (asynchronous) communication too.** Beyond live face-to-face conversation, the system should eventually support **near-real-time and batched** channels — email, messaging, and stored text/speech (compose now, send/play later). This broadens the product from live conversation to the user's fuller communication life and connects to the Product Vision's framing (today's AAC users are *locked into* near-real-time/batch; we'd be supporting those modes *well*, by choice, rather than as a limitation). Future phase. **[→ Overview To Do]**

## Keeping product documents in sync — trigger phrase "sync docs" (Ken, July 8 2026)

Product-document currency is tracked by [`DOC-SYNC.md`](DOC-SYNC.md) (any root document named `Conversant AAC *`, each stamped with the git commit it was last reviewed against — never file modification date, which a format-only edit would bump).

**Trigger phrase (Ken only): "sync docs"**, optionally followed by which documents (names or keywords). Examples: **"sync docs"** (every row not marked `✓ current` in DOC-SYNC.md), **"sync docs Product Overview"**, **"sync docs the two overviews and the user manual"**. This runs the workflow below.

**Workflow for each named document `X` (in order):**
0. **Back up first — MANDATORY.** Before modifying `X`, copy it to `Doc Backups/<X> <YYYY-MM-DD_HHMMSS>.docx`. **Never edit a product document without making a fresh backup in the same turn.** (The `.docx` live on OneDrive, which also keeps version history, but the explicit local snapshot makes any mistake trivially reversible — restore by copying the snapshot back over the working file.)
1. Read `X`'s last-reviewed commit from DOC-SYNC.md, then `git log <that commit>..HEAD` plus the CLAUDE.md / CHANGELOG entries added since — that's the change window.
2. Filter to what's relevant to `X`'s scope. **Verify any UI detail against source before asserting it** (standing working guideline) — e.g. confirm a button's label/icon in `ui.js`/`index.html`.
3. Apply the edits; verify (paragraph/content scans; render in Word if a toolchain is available).
4. **Stamp `X`'s row in DOC-SYNC.md:** Status `✓ current`, Last reviewed = today, At commit = current `HEAD`, Notes = what changed + any residual. Commit DOC-SYNC.md (the `.docx` itself stays on OneDrive, git-ignored).

**Tooling note (this dev box):** docx edits are made with `python-docx` (no pandoc / LibreOffice / zip); backups are a file copy; images/figures cannot be regenerated here, so flag figure changes as a residual in the doc's DOC-SYNC row.

**History note:** the first catch-up of the three beta-facing docs (July 8 2026) was done *before* this backup rule existed, so those three were edited in place without a pre-edit snapshot; OneDrive version history (and `Other/Previous Doc Version/` for the two overviews) is the recovery path for their pre-edit state. Every sync from now on backs up first.

## Development phases (Ken, July 8 2026) — to-do organizing convention

Ken introduced a three-stage rollout vocabulary for organizing the to-do list by *when* work must be done:
- **alpha** — **where we are now.** Users = Ken + a few user representatives (trusted, hand-held). Data and conversations are Ken's own or demo material.
- **beta** — a few **real users** (real non-speaking individuals, real communication partners, real private conversations, real API keys, on their own devices).
- **public release** — general availability.

**The gating principle:** a risk's phase is set by when it can actually bite. Anything that captures/stores a *real* third party's speech, risks *real* user data, or exposes a *real* API key becomes load-bearing at **beta** (not alpha, where the data is Ken's own and the devices are supervised). Purely architectural hardening that needs an external decision (DNS, encryption design) tends to land at **public release**.

*Scope note:* this convention is applied below to the **Security Remediation Backlog** only. The pre-existing items under **Open Questions (remaining)** and the **Overview-Document To Do List** are **not yet** re-sorted by phase — Ken floated doing that project-wide ("we might need to start organizing the to do list by development phase"). Retrofitting the whole list is a separate, deliberate pass (many judgment calls per item); do it when Ken confirms, don't silently restructure the existing lists.
