- **DECIDED (Ken, July 31 2026) — named settings profiles ARE the settings-persistence and transfer mechanism on Windows; do NOT build automatic settings-to-folder persistence.** Ken's model: **every user chooses a data folder immediately — a core setup instruction** — and is also instructed to **save their current settings as a named profile**. Thereafter, every settings change ends with either **Update** (overwrite the profile in use) or **Save as a new profile**. Either way the change lands in `<data folder>/settings/<name>.json`, which is preserved with the folder and copied to another machine like any other file. **This SUPERSEDES the bulk of the PRE-BETA "Settings persistence — data folder + per-device profiles" item below**: the automatic write-through, the portable/device-specific split, the app-minted device id and the "which device is this?" picker are **not needed** — a per-device profile is just a differently-named file in the same folder, which is the same idea with no code. *(That item's remaining value is as the record of WHY; leave it in place, cross-referenced, rather than deleting it.)*
  - **Why it works now when it wouldn't have a week ago:** the **Update** button (shipped in the current dev cycle, `c5042e0`) is what makes "re-save after every change" a single tap. Before it, re-saving meant retyping the profile's exact name and confirming an overwrite — a ritual nobody sustains, and one typo produced a second near-identical profile. The workflow depends on that button existing.
  - **It also answers the SEC-1 origin move**, which was the original motivation for automatic persistence. Moving to `app.volksswitch.org` empties `localStorage` *and* the IndexedDB folder handle, so the user re-picks their data folder (already a documented setup step) and taps **Load**. Settings come back. No migration code.
  - **The multi-device worry was overstated in the earlier design, and this is worth keeping straight.** That design split settings portable-vs-device-specific because layout values are "screen-dependent." They mostly are not: **button size, gap and minimum-gap are UNITLESS slider positions** and the conversation layout is **proportional** (v0.4.2, two-axis), so the same stored value produces the same *share of the screen* on a different device — transferring it is correct, not wrong. The genuinely per-machine values are **`voiceURI`** (a voice not installed on the new machine simply isn't found and TTS falls back to the browser default — silent but harmless) and **`keyboardMode`** (physical vs on-screen depends on that device's hardware). Both are one-tap fixes after a Load, and naming a profile per device sidesteps them entirely.
  - **THE ONE RISK, and it is the load-bearing one: a profile goes stale silently.** Change a setting, forget to Update, and the file is older than you think — with no cue anywhere on screen, because the live settings are still correct *on this machine*. The gap only surfaces at the moment you Load or move machines, i.e. the worst possible time. Ken's answer is user discipline plus instruction. **A staleness indicator is the candidate reinforcement — DEFERRED to a future decision (Ken, July 31 2026): "it may be an unnecessary complication of the UI."** *(See the TO DO under Open Questions.)*
  - **The iPad is the explicit exception (Ken's own scoping).** There the data folder is OPFS: a profile is written where the user cannot see it, cannot copy it, and where a Safari tab can have the entire origin evicted after seven days. Profiles therefore provide neither backup nor transfer on iPad, and **Export/Import stays mandatory there** — it is also the only bridge between the two iPad storage silos.
  - **Still open, and separable:** Export/Import also carries About Me, people, Express Panel, starters and conversations, all of which a folder copy already covers on Windows — so the *transfer* case for it there is now gone. What nothing else covers is an **in-app** accident: profiles snapshot settings, but nothing snapshots About Me against a mis-confirmed **Restart**. So the remaining question is narrower than before — retire Export/Import on Windows, or keep it purely as that snapshot? Decide when the setup instructions are written.
- **~~TO DO — explore SPOKEN help~~ → BUILT August 2 2026; see UI Layout Rule 17 for the shipped design. Kept below for the reasoning that led to it.** (Ken, August 2 2026.) Tap a help control, then tap the setting in question, and the app vocalizes a short explanation of it. Settings first; other surfaces later if it works.
  - **This SATISFIES UI Layout Rule 14 rather than reversing it, and that is the point.** Rule 14 removed per-control help because the only in-app alternative was a tooltip, and **a tooltip needs hover, which a direct-select user cannot produce.** Tap-then-tap needs no hover. And the cost Rule 14 was avoiding was *screen space* — audio costs none, so the panel stays exactly as decluttered as Ken asked for. The rule's text should be amended if this ships, not overturned: what it forbids is per-control **text on screen**.
  - **The machinery already exists and the audience is right.** This is an app whose user communicates *by* text-to-speech; speaking is the one output channel guaranteed to be set up and tuned to their preference. Reading is not — literacy is a stated entry requirement today, but the roadmap explicitly plans to widen to lower literacy levels, and spoken help is one of the few things that would still work there.
  - **The source text exists and is written, but in the wrong register.** `SETTINGS-HELP-TEXT.md` held all 38 removed hints, grouped by tab and already triaged into three tiers of importance; it was deleted in `22d47a1` once both manuals carried it, and is recoverable in full (221 lines) at **`git show 13fa157:SETTINGS-HELP-TEXT.md`**. **But do not speak it as written** — it was authored to be *read*, and several entries run to eighty words. Listening has no skimming and no re-reading, so spoken help needs its own much shorter register, with the manual keeping the long form. That rewrite is most of the work; the corpus is the easy part.

  **DESIGN SETTLED (Ken, August 2 2026) — text written, mechanism NOT built.**
  - **The words live in [`settings-help.json`](settings-help.json) at the project ROOT and are NOT shipped as a file.** Ken: *"I'm not thinking that we would distribute this file. The user doesn't need to be able to edit it. It is static for a release… The actual content would be embedded in the app or a supporting file inaccessible to the user."* So it follows the **`CHANGELOG.md` → `whats-new.js` pattern exactly**: a root file for editing, a generator that embeds it at release time, nothing fetched at runtime (works offline, no precache entry, no CSP surface). **The staleness trap is the same one `RELEASING.md` already warns about for release notes** — forget the generator and the embedded copy is wrong; fold a generated-matches-source check into the coverage test so it fails loudly.
  - **First cut written: 85 entries** — 59 controls (one per control in the panel), 11 tabs, 10 section headings, 5 radio groups. Median 16 words, longest 24. Keyed by element `id` (all 59 have one), radio `name`, tab `data-tab`, and a `data-help` attribute on multi-control groups. Resolution from a tap: `label[for]` → that control; else the group's `data-help`; else the group's single control.
  - **Coverage is enforced by a TEST, not a process note (my recommendation, accepted in principle).** `tests/settings-help.test.mjs` parses `index.html`, enumerates the Settings controls and fails on any missing entry or orphan — prototyped against the real file, currently 59/59 with no gaps and no orphans. This is what Ken's "include it in DOC-SYNC" asked for, done as a build failure rather than a thing to remember; add the DOC-SYNC row too, but the test is the guard that holds. Needs an ignore-list (the 12 slider steppers).
  - **The load-bearing safety property: tapping a control in help mode must NOT change it.** A slider that moves, or a checkbox that flips, while the user is asking what it does would be worse than no help at all — and this population taps imprecisely, which is the whole reason the feature is attractive. Help mode intercepts the tap (capture phase) before the control sees it, and must also suppress **focus** — focusing a text field would summon the on-screen keyboard.
  - **Barge-out, and the same-target exception (Ken).** Any tap during playback aborts the speech: *"a natural way for people to indicate that they're done early."* **Exception: tapping the SAME control or the same group aborts AND passes the action through; tapping anywhere else aborts and swallows the tap.** This fixes the dead-tap window I flagged (help is one-shot, so it has already disarmed by the time speech plays — you tap a checkbox to hear about it, then tap it again to actually change it, and without the exception that second tap is eaten). **Why it is principled and not merely convenient: the tap you can trust is the one that already landed on target deliberately.** Tapping the same group again is by construction aimed; a tap elsewhere is the one that might be a stray, and a stray is exactly what the swallow protects against.
    - **Verified safe: EVERY destructive Settings button already routes through `confirmDanger()`** — Delete/Load/Update/Save-over profile, Clear error log, and both Import and Restore (those two confirm one level down, inside `importPackageText`). So the worst a pass-through can do is raise the red confirmation card. The only unconfirmed pass-throughs are `resetUsageBtn`, `resetSizingBtn` and `pickFolderBtn`, all trivially redone or non-destructive.
    - **CARVE-OUT — sliders are excluded from pass-through.** On `<input type="range">` a tap is not a neutral action: it jumps the value to wherever the finger landed, so "abort the voice" and "set this to 90%" are the same gesture and cannot be told apart. The rule is therefore *pass-through where a tap is a DISCRETE action* (button, checkbox, select, text field, tab) and not where the tap coordinate carries the value — exactly one control type, 6 of 59. It costs nothing in practice: every slider sits in a group with **− / +** steppers, which are discrete and so pass through under the same-group rule; only a tap on the track itself is swallowed.
  - **One-shot, not sticky (Ken's call, and his reasoning is the better one):** help disarms after each control *"to avoid the situation where the user only wants to be reminded about one setting's usage and operation but can't figure out how to turn off the help message so that they can actually modify the value."* Being trapped in help mode is a worse failure for this population than an extra tap. Per **Rule 6** the armed `?` shows as selected; it also needs a visible **speaking** state so a swallowed tap reads as "still talking" rather than "broken".
  - **Tapping a TAB speaks what that tab is for and does NOT select it** (Ken) — consistent with controls not activating, and one-shot means the next tap navigates normally.
  - **Voice — intelligibility first, NOT distinctness (Ken corrected me here).** My recommendation was the free device voice, for audible distinctness from the user's own speaking voice. Ken's catch kills that: *"there's only one non-silly voice available on an iPad. If they choose the one non-silly voice as their own, then a tool tip would be read in what might be an unintelligible voice."* Correct — the measured iPadOS roster is Samantha plus 45 one-per-language `super-compact` voices plus 19 novelty voices, so "a voice that is not the user's" can easily mean Zarvox. **Rule: reuse the existing novelty-exclusion machinery (`tts.isNoveltyVoice` / the `pickPartnerVoice` selection, which already refuses to hand Practice Mode a joke voice), and where no usable DIFFERENT voice exists, fall back to the user's own rather than speaking help in a cartoon voice.** If they are on a Deepgram voice, a second Aura voice gets intelligibility and distinctness together. **Cost is explicitly not a concern** (Ken: the feature will not be used often) — do not re-raise it.
  - **Scope: Settings only** (Ken: *"until we see real value elsewhere. I don't want anything in the conversation UI that could slow or trip someone up."*) — which also matches Rule 1's priority order. The conversation screen would benefit most but is keyguard-backed, and adding a modal state there is a bigger decision.
  - **Still open:** how this interacts with the future **non-English support** to-do (the JSON would need per-language versions, like every other bundled content file).

- **TO DO — keep Apple's novelty voices out of Practice Mode's "Auto" partner pick, and split the `super-compact` tier from `compact` (measured July 31 2026; not built).** Both follow from the iPadOS voice roster recorded in the iPad section. (1) **Auto picks a gag voice on iPad.** `pickPartnerVoice()` prefers a voice that isn't the user's, in the same language; on iPadOS the only en-US voices besides Samantha are the 19 legacy `com.apple.speech.synthesis.voice.*` novelty voices, so the practice partner speaks as Albert, Zarvox or Bahh. Fix: exclude that URI prefix from Auto's candidates, and consider sorting novelty voices last in both pickers (filtering them is standard practice for web speech apps). **Decide whether to hide them outright or merely de-prioritize** — a user may legitimately want Whisper, and hiding is not reversible from the UI. (2) **`voiceQuality()` labels `super-compact` as "Compact"** (the hyphen satisfies its word boundary), so two genuinely different tiers read identically. Ascending: `super-compact < compact < enhanced < premium`. Needs a label that does not imply *better* — "Super-compact" reads as an upgrade, which is backwards. Neither is urgent; (1) is the one with a user-visible wrong result today.
  - **(1) DONE July 31 2026 — hidden, reversibly, and Auto never picks one.** Ken's call was *"hide, with a checkbox to show them."* Built in `tts.js` as `isNoveltyVoice()` / `usableVoices()`, with Settings → Speech & Input gaining "Show this device's joke voices". **Matched by EXPLICIT ID, not by the `com.apple.speech.synthesis.voice.*` prefix** — Alex lives under that same legacy prefix and is a genuinely good voice, so a prefix rule would have hidden something a user might want (unit-tested, along with Eloquence voices, which are legitimate AAC voices and must never be caught). **`pickPartnerVoice()` filters unconditionally**, whatever the checkbox says: that setting governs what the user may *choose*, not what the app picks *for* them. Verified on a simulated iPad roster — Auto now chooses **Daniel (en-GB)**, a real voice, where it previously chose Albert or Zarvox. **(2) remains open.**
    - **The "Voices offered to the app" readout was REMOVED from Settings → About (Ken, August 2 2026).** It was built the same day as the hiding, to answer "why is this voice not in my list" on a device with no console, and it did its job: the iPadOS roster is now a recorded platform fact (a fixed curated list — one `super-compact` voice per language plus the 19 legacy novelty voices, with Enhanced/Premium unreachable at any price), so re-reading the list cannot teach us anything new. It was also **not actionable by a user** — the Voice picker already shows what they can choose — and it spent a 6-row textarea plus two buttons of a panel that Rule 14 had just been used to clear out. The one user-facing question it answered, "where did the joke voices go?", is now answered directly by the "Show this device's joke voices" checkbox. **Removed outright** (Ken's call), not folded into the error-log report: `voiceDiagnosticLines`, `renderVoiceList`, the `#voiceListView`/`#voiceListActions` markup and their CSS are all gone; `tts.voiceQuality` and `tts.isNoveltyVoice` stay because the pickers use them. **What this gives up, so it is on the record:** if a beta tester on an unknown device reports "my voice isn't in the list," there is no longer any way to see what their browser actually offered — they have no console either. If that happens, the cheap restoration is to append the roster to the error-log **Copy** report, which needs to go in *ahead* of `buildErrorReport`'s early `(no errors recorded)` return, since a missing voice logs no error. **Do not rebuild it as its own panel** — it belongs in the general **"Copy System Info"** item under Open Questions (Ken, Aug 2 2026), which exists precisely because one-diagnostic-per-subsystem is how the Settings panel fills back up with developer tooling.
- **TO DO — decide whether `speakBuiltin` needs a watchdog for an utterance that never ends (found July 31 2026; PRE-EXISTING, not introduced by the voice work).** `tts.speak()` resolves on the browser's `onend`/`onerror`. If the engine accepts an utterance and then fires neither — which is exactly what a silent `speechSynthesis` on iOS looks like — the promise never settles **and `speaking` stays latched true forever**. That is worse than it sounds: the STT echo filter reads that flag, so it would conclude the app is permanently talking and **suppress every silence checkpoint from then on**, ending the conversation loop with no error raised anywhere. Reproduced in the preview (an utterance the engine accepts but never completes leaves `isSpeaking()` true indefinitely). **Partly mitigated already:** the July 31 work wrapped both the voice assignment and the `speak()` call so a *throw* can no longer latch it, and audio is unlocked on Start, which is when iOS is likeliest to swallow speech. **The remaining case is accepted-but-silent.** The candidate fix is a duration-scaled watchdog (roughly `2s + 120ms × characters`) that clears the speaking state if no end arrives — deliberately generous, because firing early is the *opposite* failure: the app would treat its own still-playing speech as the partner's. **Not built: it changes timing on the core speech path, which deserves a deliberate decision rather than a drive-by.**
- **TO DO — decide whether the settings-profile STALENESS INDICATOR is worth its UI cost (Ken, July 31 2026; deferred, not built).** The named-profile workflow (see the DECIDED entry above) has one silent failure: change a setting, forget to **Update**, and the profile file is older than you think — with no cue anywhere, because the live settings are still correct *on this machine*. It surfaces only when you Load or move machines, i.e. the worst moment. **The candidate fix:** on Settings open, diff the live settings bundle against the active profile's file and say so in the status line that already exists — `In use: "Ken's Surface" — unsaved changes`. **Deferred on Ken's reservation: "it may be an unnecessary complication of the UI."** The Settings panel is already dense, and for this population a line most users read as noise most of the time is a cost.
  - **Two details to have in hand if it is revisited.** (1) The note would appear *only* when the live settings actually differ from the saved profile, so a user who keeps the Update habit never sees it — a transient prompt rather than a standing label, which is a distinction easy to lose over time. (2) The failure it addresses is asymmetric: forgetting to Update costs nothing today and everything at transfer time, and cannot be spotted by looking at the screen. Against that, it can only ever prompt someone who was about to do the right thing anyway.
  - **What would change the picture:** a real instance of a stale profile biting — settings lost on a machine move, or a tester restoring an out-of-date profile. Until that happens the risk is theoretical while the UI cost is certain. **Trivial to build when wanted** (`exportSettingsBundle()` vs the profile file's `settings`, rendered by the existing `setProfileStatus`) — no new controls, no new layout, nothing that moves a keyguard hole. Ken's observation that prompted the July 31 backup work: Export/Import looks redundant on Windows, where the data folder already gives you backup, transfer and ownership for free. **The premise is currently half true, and that half is the whole justification:** the four user-owned data files and the conversation logs *are* in the folder, but the **live settings are not** — they sit in origin-scoped `localStorage` (`storage.js` `loadSettings`/`saveSettings`), and only an explicitly-saved **named profile** ever writes a settings snapshot to `settings/<name>.json`. So copying a data folder to a second machine today carries About Me, people, Express Panel and starters, and **silently loses every setting** (voice, dock side, keyboard/Express layout, button size, gaps, text sizes, silence period, placeholder delays, tap mode, cards-per-category). Export is the only thing that moves them without the manual profile ritual.
  - **Why this becomes a real question:** once the **PRE-BETA "Settings persistence — data folder + per-device profiles"** work lands (see that section), settings live in the folder like everything else, copying the folder carries them, and Export/Import's *transfer* job on Windows largely dissolves. **That is the moment to decide** — deciding now would be deciding against a premise that is about to change.
  - **What would still argue for keeping it on Windows** (weigh these, don't assume they win): a user who never granted a data folder (everything is `localStorage`-only for them); a **single file** you can email for support or park on a different machine/drive, versus talking someone through copying a folder tree; and protection against **in-app** destruction (a Restart, a reset, a bad import) that a folder copy only protects against if the user remembered to make one. Note the July-31 change moved backups *into* the data folder on Windows, so as built it now guards in-app mistakes but **not** loss of the folder itself — the user must still copy the file out.
  - **Options when it's decided:** keep as-is · keep Export/Import but drop the *settings* payload on Windows once the folder carries them (would make an exported package non-portable across the two platforms — probably wrong) · make the whole feature iPad-only (where it is not optional: OPFS is invisible, a Safari tab is refused persistent storage, and the two iPad modes are separate storage silos) · retire it on Windows entirely. **Do not narrow it before the settings-persistence work ships**, or a Windows user loses the only settings-transfer path they have.

The browser/hardware layer is covered by the manual procedure `tests/MANUAL-TEST-PLAN.md` + seed fixtures `tests/fixtures/` (a "Repeatable setup" section resets to a known baseline: fixture data folder + fixed partner script). **The open question:** build a headless-browser E2E tier (Playwright — or the already-present Puppeteer dep) that drives the *real* app with a **fake `SpeechRecognition` + stubbed `fetch`** injected, to convert the DOM-flow manual cases (user-started flow through the UI, Express Panel, composer/keyboard, Settings) into repeatable automated tests — shrinking the manual surface to only the genuinely physical cases (real mic acoustics, the echo loop, TTS timing, keyguard fit, FSA folder writes, the auto-update deploy). Medium build; decide go/no-go later. This session also extracted the conversation-loop decision logic + the silent-dead-end tripwires out of `app.js` into `app/js/conversation-logic.js` so they're unit-tested (100% coverage), and added the tripwires that log any "complete turn but no options" / "non-COMPLETE while user leading" outcome (red-wash + errors.log) so the July-2026 stall class can never be silent again. **[relates to [[test-harness]] in machine memory]**
- **TO DO — decide the disposition of the "amber box" (`showResponseError`) before public release (Ken, July 8 2026; not built).** On a *thrown* generation error (`generateOptions`/`regenerate`/`reframe` — API/parse failure), the Response Palette is currently replaced by a worded box — the literal error string (e.g. "Couldn't get responses: 429 Too Many Requests") + a manual **"Try again"** button (`ui.showResponseError`, [ui.js](app/js/ui.js), rendered into `#responseOptions`; styles `.response-error*`). This conflicts with Ken's "no error messages in the UI — they mean nothing to the user and just confuse" stance. It was **left in place for now** (v0.5.74's faint-red transcript wash is the new non-verbal heads-up for *all* logged errors, thrown and silent). **Decide before public release:** most likely **remove `showResponseError` entirely** and rely on the transcript wash + **silent automatic retry-with-backoff** for the dominant transient API 429/529 case (recorded as the likely real fix for the demo "no options" failure), leaving the response cards simply empty until a retry succeeds. Note: the transcript wash already covers the *silent* no-usable-responses paths (empty/malformed `responses[]`, INCOMPLETE classification, superseded generation token) that never throw and never showed the box anyway — so removing the box loses no coverage. Ken has **never actually seen the box** because generation hasn't thrown in his testing. Touches `ui.js` (drop `showResponseError` + `.response-error*` CSS), `app.js` (the four `showResponseError` call sites → wash-only + optional retry).
- **TO DO (documentation, both manuals) — explain the browser's save prompt on Export, and that nothing leaves the device (Ken, August 1 2026).** Exporting from a Safari tab on the iPad raises Safari's own download confirmation — *"Do you want to download 'conversant-backup-….json' on 'volksswitch.github.io'?"* — which reads as though the data is being stored at, or sent to, that site. **It is not.** [`downloadText`](app/js/data-transfer.js:137) builds the file in memory from local storage, wraps it in a `Blob`, and hands a **`blob:` URL** to the browser; there is no network request at any point in an export, and the same file would be produced with the iPad in airplane mode. **Two things collide here and NEITHER is ours to reword** — the dialog names the origin of the *page that initiated* the save (the page's identity, not the file's source), and the browser calls every file handoff a **"download"** because that is its only mechanism for getting a file out of a page. So our **Export** button and Safari's "download" describe the same act from opposite directions, and no web API reaches the dialog's text.
  - **Ken's call: do NOT put this in the app.** A hint under the button is fine print nobody reads; it belongs in the manuals.
  - **Document the round trip too — fully supported, but manual at both ends.** The save sheet puts the file wherever the user directs it (Downloads by default on iPad), and **Import from a file…** opens the Files picker so they can navigate back to it. What is **not** possible, and will not become possible, is either direction happening automatically, or the app listing what is in Downloads: iOS gives a web app no directory picker and no durable handle to a user-visible folder — the same limitation that forced OPFS for the data folder. So every backup out and every restore in costs exactly one user pick, permanently.
  - **UNVERIFIED, and it decides how prominent this passage needs to be:** whether the **Home Screen app** raises the same origin-naming dialog or a plain share sheet with no site name. Standalone mode has no address bar, so this may be a browser-tab-only artifact. **Ken expects most users to run from the Home Screen** (better use of screen real estate), so check it on the device before writing.
  - **Also observed, and separate:** the dialog named **`volksswitch.github.io`**, not `conversant.volksswitch.org` — so that iPad tab was on the **pre-August-1 origin**. Storage is per-origin, so iPad data held at the old address is invisible at the new one; the crossing is Export there, Import here. Worth confirming why the redirect isn't reaching the device (a service worker cached at the old origin will keep serving offline-first without ever hitting it) — the manuals now cite the new address.
  - *(Standing rule: any part that also applies to Chromium — where an export with no data folder chosen likewise goes down the download path — must land in **both** manuals in the same pass.)*
- **TO DO — how do we obtain a user's CONFIGURATION during beta and after release? Probably a "Copy System Info" button (Ken, August 2 2026; think it through, not yet designed).** **⚠ ANSWERED August 7 2026 — see "Beta instrumentation and error reporting."** Resolved by its own preferred option (a): system info **extends the existing error report** rather than becoming a competing button, it rides the same automatic channel, and it is where the removed "Voices offered to the app" readout returns. What is *added* to the analysis below: the device facts no web API can reach — model, physical screen size, **whether a keyguard is fitted**, mount, physical keyboard — which need a one-time "About your setup" field stamped into every report. Keyguard yes/no is the one that most changes how a layout complaint should be read. Ken: *"think about how we can obtain configuration information from the user during beta and after release — maybe it's a 'Copy System Info' button that grabs everything relevant to the app."* **The problem is structural and permanent, not a beta inconvenience:** this app has **no backend and no telemetry by design**, which is the same property that lets it outlive the funded AAC projects that were shelved — so the *only* channel for learning anything about a user's setup is the user themselves, on a tablet with no console, pasting something into a message. Everything we might want to ask them ("which dock are you on?", "what does your browser report?") is a question they should not have to answer by hand.
  - **This generalizes what we just deleted, and that is the useful frame.** The "Voices offered to the app" readout (removed Aug 2 2026, above) was one narrow slice of exactly this — a copy-to-clipboard diagnostic for one subsystem, built ad hoc when a specific question came up. Building one of those per subsystem is how a Settings panel fills back up with developer tooling; **one general button is the right shape.** The removal note's "cheap restoration" (append the voice roster to a report) folds into this item rather than standing alone.
  - **Candidate contents, all already computable in-app:** app version + `BUILD_ID`; platform verdict from `platform.js` (engine, standalone-vs-tab, `speechRecognitionSupport`); the `viewport.js` metric set (layout viewport, screen, `devicePixelRatio`, zoom state); storage backend + whether a folder is granted + `navigator.storage.persisted()`; the STT/TTS provider choices; the full settings bundle; dock/layout/button-size/gap values; the voice roster; and the error log.
  - **The load-bearing design questions — this is what needs THINKING, not typing.** (a) **One button or fold into the existing error-log Copy report?** `buildErrorReport` already exists, is already the thing we ask a tester to paste, and already bundles version + transcripts — a second button competing with it is a worse outcome than extending it. (b) **What is the delivery channel** — clipboard paste into an email, a downloaded file, or an upload on the planned issue-reporting page (see the item below; these two are really one design). (c) **How does the user see what they are sending?** The issue-reporting note already commits to explicit + previewable, and configuration is less sensitive than a transcript but not nothing. (d) **What must NEVER be in it:** the API keys (SEC-6 — the same exclusion the settings profiles enforce) and any partner speech (SEC-2, which `logError` already redacts for a private conversation).
  - **Distinguish this from the Usage Instrumentation section above:** that one is about *behavior* (is the app being used, and which features), this one is about *configuration* (what is this user's setup). Same delivery channel, same privacy rules, different payload — and the configuration half is the cheaper and more immediately useful of the two, because it is a pure snapshot with no ongoing recording.
  - **Gate: beta**, alongside the issue-reporting page and SEC-7 — in alpha Ken is holding the device.
- **TO DO — dedicated issue-reporting web page, optionally with transcript upload (Ken, July 3 2026, User-Manual comment 28; not built — outside the app).** **⚠ PARTLY OVERTAKEN by "Beta instrumentation and error reporting" (Ken, August 7 2026):** automatic sending to a Ken-owned endpoint is now the primary channel and default-on, so this page is no longer the main route in — but it remains the right home for a tester-initiated report with an attached transcript, which stays **manual and previewable** because automatic payloads never carry speech. Ken wants a web page specifically for reporting issues (today the manual §12 just links to the generic `volksswitch.org/index.php/contact-us/`). Open question he raised: should a beta tester be able to **upload the specific conversation transcript** where the problem occurred? *My take (recorded for when this is built):* yes, and it's low-effort to support because conversation logs are already JSON files in the user's data folder (`conversations/<timestamp>.json`) — the report form just needs a file-picker that accepts one of those. Caveats to design in: (a) transcripts can contain private content, so uploading must be **explicit + previewable** (show what's being sent) and honor the new per-conversation "Don't save" choice (a not-saved conversation has no file to attach); (b) strip or flag the partner's raw audio-derived text vs. cleaned; (c) include the app version + the viewport-metrics log (`viewport.js`) automatically for context. This is Volksswitch web-infrastructure work, not app code — the app side is at most an "Export this conversation for a bug report" affordance. Pairs with the existing "include the version number when reporting" guidance.

Use "placeholder" going forward in code, UI, and docs. Exception kept: the grid-spacer "filler" in `keyboard.js`/`keyboard-layouts.js`/`styles.css` is a different sense (an inert layout spacer) and stays. CLAUDE.md's historical log entries keep the old word where they describe past work; that's history, not current terminology.

Keep the two role pools (first filler acknowledges, later ones say "still thinking"). Joins placeholders / Express items / openers-closers as the configurable, data-folder-portable sets.
- **DONE (Ken, July 8 2026) — placeholders now play after ANY complete partner turn, reversing the v0.3.9 "questions only" gate.** Rationale: a placeholder is a **social-presence signal** — if the partner hears *nothing* after they speak (even a greeting), they may assume the user didn't hear or can't communicate. `shouldPlayPlaceholder` (`app.js`) now returns true for every COMPLETE turn; only two cases still stay silent: **INCOMPLETE** (partner still talking) and **is_repair_initiator** ("What?/Huh?" — the partner is asking the USER to repeat, which is the instant repair-of-self flow, where a "let me think" beat before re-speaking reads wrong). *(Repair-initiator exclusion is a judgement call — flag if Ken wants it firing too.)* Because it now fires after greetings/statements, "Good question." would be absurd there, so the **acknowledgment pool split into two sub-pools**: `placeholders.json` `acknowledgment: { question:[], general:[] }` — `question` (warm "Good question.") for `partner_action ∈ {QUESTION, INVITATION, REQUEST}` (the `QUESTION_ACTIONS` set), `general` ("Let me see." / "One moment." / "Just a second.") for everything else. `app.js` passes `placeholders.start({ question })`; `placeholders.js` `normalizePools` is backward-compatible (a legacy flat array or flat `acknowledgment` array fills both sub-pools). The initial-delay, the per-turn cap, and cap=0 (off) all still apply (a quick pick plays none). *Greeting phrasing is imperfect (no phrase is truly natural before a greeting reply) and tunable once the placeholder editor lands.*
- **DONE (Ken, July 8 2026) — repair-of-self pre-generates rephrase + expand so all three cards show real text.** When the partner asks the user to repeat ("What?"), the REPAIR-OF-SELF palette used to show **re-speak** with the user's actual last words (in-hand, instant) but **rephrase**/**expand** as mere hints ("Say it differently" / "Explain it more"), each requiring an AI round-trip *after* selection. Now, on entering repair-of-self, `app.js prefetchRepairOptions(token)` fires **one** combined call — `llm.repairOptions(lastUtterance, history)` → `{ rephrase, expand }` (new `parseRepairOptions`) — and `engine.setRepairOptions({rephrase,expand})` fills those two palette entries' `text` (and flips their `latency` to `instant`); the palette re-renders so all three cards show real, immediately-speakable wording. `handleRepairOfSelf` prefers the pre-generated `response.text` and only falls back to the on-demand `llm.repairSelf` round-trip if the prefetch hasn't arrived / failed. Guarded by the `generationToken` and a `mode === REPAIR_OF_SELF` check so a superseded turn or an already-made pick can't clobber. **Cost:** one extra LLM call per "What?" event even if the user picks re-speak — accepted (repair-of-self is infrequent, user funds tokens, and it's one combined call, not two). `llm.repairSelf` is kept as the fallback path.

- **PINNED — directional gaps + button-size handling in the CONSTRAINED direction (Ken, June 29 2026; design unfinished, NOT built).** Follow-on to the v0.5.41–v0.5.44 button-size/gap work. Ken's refinements to capture before resuming: **(1) Gaps need NOT be equal H vs V** in the keyboard / Express Panel. The "Button spacing" slider should grow the gap only in the direction the dock can **expand** (horizontal for a side dock — it widens into the panel; vertical for a bottom dock — it grows into the transcript). **(2)** In the **constrained** direction (vertical for a side dock = full-height column; horizontal for a bottom dock = full-width strip) the gap can't grow without shrinking the buttons. **(3) Precedence: minimum gap › button size › spacing.** Add a **"Minimum gap" setting** (new slider) that is a hard floor on the gap in every direction and **outranks button size** — i.e. button size can't increase (in a given direction) if doing so would push that direction's gap below the minimum; button size can always expand in an unrestricted direction. So at max button size the constrained-direction gap sits at the minimum and the buttons stop growing rather than shrink; **reducing button size frees room for the constrained-direction gap to grow**. **(4) Command Bar:** increasing the gap should also open space **above and below** the Command Bar buttons (taken from the transcript), not just left/right. **OPEN (Ken is to define): the simple rule for how gap + button size resolve in the constrained direction** — specifically what fills the leftover once buttons are at their set size (gap absorbs it / gap caps at the spacing value with empty space / buttons fill). Ken: "I need to come up with a simple rule." A clean CSS path exists (clamp()/calc() keyed off `--kbd-rows`/`--kbd-cols` + `100vh`/`100vw` for the constrained extent), and a `--gap-min` token + a Minimum-gap slider, but **do not build until Ken supplies the rule.** *(An inert token scaffold `--gap-cross`/`--gap-h`/`--gap-v` was prototyped then reverted to keep the tree clean.)*
- **TO DO — define what each Command Bar button can OVERRIDE / interrupt (Ken, June 29 2026; not built):** with the controls consolidated into the Command Bar (v0.5.40), we need to specify, per button, what state it interrupts when pressed mid-action — especially when a **modal action is open**. Ken's example: if the user has opened **"In my own words"** (the composer modal overlaying the response footprint, keyboard over the dock) and then presses **"Wind down"**, the composer should be **dismissed and the standard base UI restored** (the response palette / Response Palette reservation reappears, keyboard hidden) before the Wind-down (pre-closing) flow runs. So a Command Bar press is not just "do my action" — it may first need to **tear down whatever modal/transient state is active** and return to the base screen. This generalizes: which Command Bar buttons cancel the composer modal? which cancel an in-flight generation? which are no-ops vs. which seize the floor? Tie into UI Layout **Rule 8** (modal-action assets superimpose over inert base elements) — the inverse question is which base controls stay *live* during a modal and what they do to it. Produce a small matrix (button × current-state → effect) and wire the teardown consistently. Pairs with the existing engine override handlers (`handleWindDown`/`handleInitiate`/etc.) which currently assume the base screen is showing.
- **DONE (v0.5.41) — user-facing button-size + spacing settings (Ken, June 29 2026).** Built as **two unitless sliders** (Settings → Speech & Input): **"Minimum button size"** drives `--btn-min-dim`; **"Button spacing"** drives a new `--grid-gap` (= keyguard bar width). Resolved the open questions: **(1) one global scalar** (Ken: "affects all buttons"); per-region sizing per Rule 5 (bigger for more-frequent actions) is a deferred refinement a single scalar can't express. **(2) Content buttons reconcile by GROWING THE DOCK, not flooring the keys:** `--dock-w`/`--dock-h` = `min(cap, max(floor, rows/cols × --btn-min-dim + gaps + pad))`, so the bottom dock grows taller / side dock grows wider just enough that keys clear the minimum (the v0.5.27 overflow worry is gone — the container yields, not the key). **(3) Unitless slider** (not presets/mm — mm is unenforceable without real DPI). **(4) Setup-tier** (moves keyguard holes). The transcript is the shrink victim (3rem floor); caps (72vh/70vw) keep the main area usable. **This UNBLOCKS the word-prediction placement** (next item) — the button-size questions Ken wanted settled first are now settled.
- **TO DO — explore a "phrase expansion" (abbreviation) feature (Ken, June 29 2026; not built):** let the user type a short abbreviation that auto-expands to a longer phrase on a word boundary — e.g. **"btw" → "by the way"**, "omw" → "on my way", "ty" → "thank you". This is a **fully local, deterministic, user-configurable** map (no AI, no network — like the rest of the local prediction tier) — distinct from both (a) the inline word *prediction*/completion (v0.5.42, which completes a prefix to a single real word) and (b) the LLM-driven "AI-assisted composition" (few words → a full sentence in the user's voice). **Two behaviors per entry, because some abbreviations expand and others should just be *spoken* differently (Ken, June 29 2026):** (i) **text expansion** — replace the typed abbreviation in the box with the full phrase ("btw" → "by the way"), which changes both what's displayed and what's spoken; (ii) **spoken-form override** — the displayed text stays the abbreviation but TTS says it differently, e.g. **"lol" should be spoken as the initialism "L-O-L"** (spelled out), not read as the word "lol" or expanded to "laughing out loud". Behavior (ii) is exactly the **[[display-text-vs-spoken-text]] / pronunciation-lexicon** TODO (display form ≠ spoken form, applied as a TTS-time substitution) — so the abbreviation feature and the pronunciation lexicon likely share one user-owned map/editor: each entry has a display form + an optional expansion (rewrites the box) and/or an optional spoken form (overrides TTS), with "spell it out letter-by-letter" as a built-in spoken mode for initialisms. **Natural seam:** the same word-boundary handling in `keyboard.js` where a separator is typed — when the just-typed "word" exactly matches an abbreviation in the map, replace it with the expansion before inserting the separator (mirrors how `acceptGhost()` already rewrites the word at the boundary). **Design questions to settle:** (1) it should be a **user-editable set** (joins placeholders / Express items / openers-closers as a configurable, data-folder-portable list with its own editor); (2) case handling (match case-insensitively? preserve/capitalize the expansion to match?); (3) whether an expansion can itself be a multi-word phrase with punctuation; (4) interaction with the inline ghost (an abbreviation like "btw" shouldn't also show a misleading word-completion ghost — probably suppress the ghost when the prefix matches a known abbreviation, or show the expansion AS the ghost); (5) ship a small starter set of common abbreviations as the default. Likely a small, high-value patch once the editor pattern is reused. **[relates to [[inline word prediction]] and the AI-assisted-composition build-order item]**
- **DONE (v0.5.42) — word prediction returns as an INLINE GHOST, not buttons (Ken, June 29 2026).** Resolved the "where do prediction buttons live" problem by NOT using buttons: the completion shows **inline in the text box** (bold, tinted, tunable color) and is accepted by typing **any word-separator** (space/comma/period/…), so it costs zero grid space, needs no keyguard alignment, and has no moving tap target. Built in `keyboard.js` (non-interactive mirror overlay `#predGhost`) — see the v0.5.42 row. The old `.kbd-preds` button overlay stays hidden/unused (infra preserved). *Remaining/optional:* prediction for physical-keyboard typists (the ghost is currently on-screen-keyboard only); showing >1 candidate (inline is single-suggestion by nature).
- **FUTURE — non-English language support (Ken, June 27 2026; not built).** Conversant AAC will support languages other than English in a future version. No architecture audit is needed before then — the modular design means adding language support is mostly content/configuration work, not structural change. Known touchpoints to revisit when this feature is scoped: (1) **STT** — `SpeechRecognition.lang` needs to become a user-settable language preference (`stt.js`; currently defaults to the browser's language); (2) **TTS** — voice selection already exposes language-tagged voices via `speechSynthesis.getVoices()`; the picker just needs language grouping; (3) **LLM prompt** — `llm.js` `buildProfileBlock()` needs a language instruction ("respond in Spanish") when a non-English language is configured — a prompt addition, not a structural change; (4) **Hardcoded content** — `placeholders.json`, Express Panel defaults (`express-items.js`), openers/closers (`engine.js`), and worldview question text (`worldview-questions.json`) would each need per-language versions; the files/seams exist; (5) **Word prediction** — `data/words.json` is English-only; non-English languages need their own frequency lists (`prediction.js` is otherwise language-agnostic); (6) **Keyboard layouts** — the layout data structure already supports arbitrary key arrangements; non-Latin scripts need new layout data, not code changes; (7) **UI chrome** — all button labels, settings names, and hint text are hardcoded English strings; full UI i18n would require extracting these to a localization file (a UI pass, not an architecture change). *No audit required now; revisit this list when the feature is formally scoped.*
- **FUTURE — context-sensitive page sets for fast phrases / partners / feelings (Ken, June 26 2026; not built):** swap the fast-phrase panel's content based on *context* — where I am, who I'm talking with, or what I'm doing — so the phrases (and the partner/feeling influencers) on offer fit the situation. A natural extension of the user-editable fast-phrase sets + the Partner/Feeling toggles (v0.5.14). Connects to Phase-2 situational awareness (GPS / partner recognition / calendar) as the context source, and to the editor (a "page set" becomes a named, context-bound layout the user defines). Record now; build after the editor + situational awareness land.
  - **CONFIRMED as a direction and named (Ken, August 5 2026): Express Panel content — or a portion of it — becomes a function of place, person, or both, and that is what adds "ONE-CLICK FRAMING" to the panel.** The naming matters because it settles the tension found the same day (see the situational-awareness-vs-framing entry): **the place and partner toggles never become framing controls — they stay "where I am / who I'm facing."** Framing arrives one level down, as an explicit tap on a phrase that the context *surfaced*. The user is at Pulp Comics, so a comics phrase is on the panel; tapping it is a deliberate act of raising the topic, and the location signal is never re-purposed. This is why the situational reading can be hardened in the prompt without closing off the framing need.
  - **Two kinds of context-surfaced button, and they must not be conflated at build time.** A context **phrase** *speaks* (the existing `speakAsUserTurn` path — it takes the floor and commits). A context **framing chip** *steers* — it says nothing aloud and instead re-generates the response cards around a topic, on the already-built Reframe seam (`llm.generateResponses({steer})` → `engine.refreshPalette`), exactly as the closed-set choice chips do. "One-click framing" is the second; both probably want to exist.
  - **Rule 1 licenses this, via the same reasoning as the July 2026 choice chips.** It changes *what occupies* a cell (Spatial Stability requirement 2, the weaker one) and not the grid (requirement 1), so the keyguard is untouched. Reuse the choice-chip mechanism outright: leading cells claimed dynamically, standing phrases shift along, an equal number falls off the end, and a Settings ceiling stops a long context set from eating the panel.
  - **The real conflict to design for: choice chips and context chips both want the LEADING cells**, and a closed set can arrive at any moment while context phrases are showing. Needs an explicit priority (my expectation: the partner's own offered alternatives outrank standing context, consistent with "the partner's alternatives outrank the fillers"), and a rule for whether context items are displaced or merely pushed along.
  - **Data model seam:** Express Panel items gain an optional scope (`placeId` / `personId` / both), so a scoped item renders only when that influencer is active. Fits the existing `express-panel.json` item list without restructuring it; a scoped item is invisible in the editor's panel preview unless the scope is simulated, which is a UX question the editor pass has to answer.
  - **Note the two influencers change at different rhythms** — a place persists across End conversation (ending a conversation does not move you) while a partner is cleared by it — so a place-scoped set is stable across a visit and a person-scoped set turns over per conversation.
- **TO DO — multiple options per move slot (Ken, June 19 2026; not built):** let the AI optionally provide more than one PREFERRED / DISPREFERRED / INITIATIVE option. This changes the **four-slot, positionally-stable palette** (preferred always first, repair always last) that is fixed for motor automaticity, so it needs a deliberate design choice between two shapes: **(a) a configurable count per slot** (e.g. 2 PREFERRED, 1 DISPREFERRED) rendered as a grouped/expandable palette — more powerful but erodes the stable-geometry invariant; or **(b) a per-slot "more like this" expander** that keeps the four-slot geometry and reveals extra options on demand. Recommendation leans (b) to preserve the invariant. Pairs with the **whole-palette "Show me different options" regenerate** shipped in v0.3.16 (regenerate replaces the set; this adds *more within* a slot). Decide the shape before building; touches `llm.js` (moves schema / count), `engine.js` (`paletteFromMoves` priority/ordering), and `ui.js` (rendering).
- **TO DO — pronunciation: display vs. spoken text for proper nouns + static statements (Ken, June 16 2026; not built):** browser voices (Google + Microsoft) mispronounce proper nouns and ignore prosody punctuation, so the only usable text-level fix is **phonetic respelling** — which means the displayed string and the spoken string must diverge. Build: (1) a **pronunciation lexicon** (display-form → spoken respelling) applied as a **TTS-time substitution on every utterance**, so names are corrected everywhere they're spoken — including inside LLM-generated responses the user never typed; (2) **`{ display, speak }` capture UI** for proper-noun answers in About Me + the People editor (testable in-flow via "Speak my answer"); (3) the same `{ display, speak }` pair for static statements (app-provided: placeholders, "Please repeat what you said.", openers/closers, ACK tokens, `placeholders.json`; and user-embedded custom phrases), `speak` falling back to `display`. Open question to settle first: per-field pairs feeding a global map vs. a standalone user-managed pronunciation dictionary. Reuses the **Response Option Display** compressed-display/expanded-speech model. Full spec in the **Display text vs. spoken text** bullet under the TTS-inflection item below. Smallest first build: the TTS-time substitution step + a simple lexicon (pays off immediately for both questionnaire read-back and generated responses).
- **TO DO — single-instance enforcement (approach decided, not built; Ken, June 16 2026):** prevent multiple live instances/launches of the app. Approach: **Web Locks API** (`navigator.locks.request` for an exclusive lifetime-held lock → later tabs/windows show a blocking "already running" screen instead of starting mic/engine) + **PWA manifest `launch_handler: { client_mode: "focus-existing" }`** (re-launching the installed app focuses the running window). Why it matters: two instances feed each other's mics with their own TTS (cross-instance feedback the v0.3.4 content-echo filter can't catch) and race on the FSA data-folder writes. Boundary: web APIs can't coordinate across different browsers/profiles (one-browser dedicated device assumed; an FSA folder lock-file is the only cross-browser option, heavier). Full spec in the **Single-instance enforcement** subsection under the v0.3.4 Test-suite Pass 1 section.
- **Relationship data restructure — BUILT (v0.2.27):** `relationships.json` graph model, People editor in About Me, LLM injection. See the **Relationship Data** section above. Remaining refinements: per-person richer attrs, person↔person edge editing in the UI, and confirming on-disk persistence on the tablet.
- **Conversation & relationship goals — design recorded, not built:** three-layer model (disposition → standing relationship goal → conversation goal) mapping to worldview Tier-B / `relationships.json` edges / session runtime; expression = curated menu + free text. See the **Conversation & Relationship Goals** section above. Standing relationship goals are the smallest first build (slot onto the existing graph edge + People editor).
- Response option UI layout: `UI-Design.docx` now specifies the four-region layout and move palette; remaining question is screen allocation between traditional AAC vocabulary and the AI-facilitated regions (composer-access region is the integration point; ultimately user-configurable per the Configuration Model)
- Worldview questionnaire: design complete and the five open decisions resolved (June 14 2026); **Build Steps 1–4 implemented and verified — core worldview feature complete** (data model + registry, questionnaire UI, LLM integration, interim Name/About You removed). See the **Worldview Questionnaire (June 2026)** section. Remaining steps (5 expand Tier-B/C content, 6 RAG-lite) are enhancements.
- Placeholder utterances: currently drawn randomly from a static JSON file (app/data/placeholders.json). Predictable fillers will become a joke to communication partners over time. LLM-generated contextual fillers would sound more natural and could acknowledge the topic, but must be evaluated against token cost impact. The architecture already supports user-funded API keys, so any added cost is borne by the user. *Design now specified:* the filler ladder in `Conversation-Engine-Design.docx` §6 (rung 1 static token ≤1 s, rung 2 static-or-contextual, rung 3 re-fill); contextual fillers are the rung-2 LLM option in the Configuration Model. Remaining work is implementation and the token-cost evaluation.
- TTS inflection: browser TTS has limited control (pitch, rate, volume on SpeechSynthesisUtterance). Natural-sounding placeholder delivery matters — monotone fillers will sound robotic. Voice banking / cloned voices (future) would give much better inflection control. Evaluate whether pitch/rate tweaks on fillers can improve naturalness in the near term.
  - **SSML does NOT work through the browser Web Speech API (finding, Ken's question June 16 2026).** The Windows built-in (SAPI/OneCore) and Google Chrome voices we use are driven via `SpeechSynthesisUtterance`, which has **no SSML support** — passing `<speak>`/`<prosody>`/`<break>` markup as the utterance text is read literally or stripped, not parsed (Chrome has never supported SSML here; don't depend on it). So inserting SSML "modifier characters" into a phrase to control inflection/timing is not an option with the current stack. **What actually works in-browser, three levers:** (1) **per-utterance** `pitch` (0–2), `rate` (0.1–10), `volume` (0–1) — set on the whole utterance, not mid-phrase; (2) **split a phrase into multiple chained utterances** each with different pitch/rate to fake an inflection contour (crude but the only way to vary prosody within a sentence); (3) **punctuation** — commas / periods / ellipses / em-dashes genuinely affect pause length and some intonation in most engines (this is the real "insert characters" answer), and `?`/`!` nudge intonation on some voices (engine-dependent, subtle). **Full SSML** (`<prosody rate/pitch>`, `<break time>`, `<emphasis>`, `<say-as>`) requires a **cloud TTS engine** — Google Cloud Text-to-Speech, Azure Speech, or Amazon Polly — which is a different architecture (network + per-character cost, user-funded like the LLM) and would plug into the same swappable TTS abstraction as voice banking. Near-term naturalness work is therefore limited to levers 1–3; richer control is the cloud-TTS / voice-banking future track.
  - **Reference doc:** `Shaping-Speech-Through-Text.docx` (generator `generate-tts-pronunciation-doc.js`) — practical per-character guide (punctuation for pacing/intonation, characters that change pronunciation, the per-voice caveat, a testing protocol via the "Speak my answer" button, and placeholder before/after candidates). Regenerable on request.
  - **EMPIRICAL FINDING (Ken tested, June 16 2026): punctuation/special characters for *prosody* are IGNORED by both the Google and Microsoft browser voices.** Commas/periods/ellipses/em-dashes did not produce usable pause or intonation changes on the voices we ship with — so **lever 3 (punctuation for pacing/intonation) is effectively a dead end** with the current stack. The doc's Section 3 is therefore over-optimistic for these engines and should be de-emphasized when next regenerated. **What remains usable at the text level is pronunciation, not prosody: phonetic respelling (and syllable hyphenation) of words the voice says wrong** — this is the lever Ken is investigating for proper nouns. Prosody control (pauses, pitch contour) is left to the code levers (per-utterance pitch/rate/volume, phrase-splitting) and, for real control, the cloud-TTS future track.
- **Display text vs. spoken text — needs TWO properties, and it extends to proper nouns in user data (Ken, June 16 2026; NOT built).** A consequence of the pronunciation work above: when a word is shown one way but must be *said* another way (phonetic respelling so the voice pronounces it correctly), the displayed string and the spoken string diverge. This applies in two places:
  - **Static UI statements** (app-provided AND user-embedded): placeholders, "Please repeat what you said.", "Sorry, what was that?", "Hold on, let me think.", the openers/closers, the rung-1 ACK tokens, `app/data/placeholders.json`, and any user-authored static text (custom starters/fillers, canned phrases) → each becomes a **`{ display, speak }` pair** (clean text shown; respelled text spoken), `speak` falling back to `display` when absent.
  - **Proper nouns in About Me / relationship data (Ken's June 16 2026 extension):** names of people, places, pets, organizations entered in the questionnaire and the People editor are exactly the words browser voices mispronounce. Each proper-noun answer should carry a spoken form alongside its displayed form (testable right there via the in-flow "Speak my answer" button).
  - **This is the same pattern already specified for response options** (compressed *display* hint vs. expanded *spoken* text — see **Response Option Display**); generalize that one model, don't invent a second.
  - **Design note / my recommendation (raise before building):** proper nouns differ from static strings in one way that matters — they also appear inside **LLM-generated responses the user never typed** (a generated reply that names a friend). A per-field `{display, speak}` fixes the *displayed answer* but NOT the generated occurrence. The robust mechanism is a **pronunciation lexicon** (display-form → spoken respelling) applied as a **TTS preprocessing substitution on every utterance** — so one entry corrects the name everywhere it is spoken (questionnaire read-back, generated responses, starters). Per-field pairs are the *capture UI* for that lexicon. Open question: keep both (per-field pair feeds a global map) vs. a standalone user-managed pronunciation dictionary. Touches placeholders module + JSON, engine OPENER/CLOSER palettes, hard-coded prompt strings in `app.js`/`ui.js`, the worldview + relationships data models, and a TTS-time substitution step. Not built — record now so it's designed in when those strings/fields are next touched.
